2419. CHARLES SPINK , stealing 1 plough and I saw, value 16 s.; the goods of William Clark; and 2 saws, 10 s.; the goods of Philip Wilson.
WILLIAM CLARK . I have a shop at Maryland Point, Stratford—it was broken open, and I lost some tools, on 14th Oct.—these are my tools.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What is the prisoner? A. A fellow-workman of mine—he had not the key of the shop—I left the tools safe on Saturday night, just after six o'clock, and the shop locked up—I left no one there.
TITUS PESZUKSKI . I met the prisoner with some tools, on the Saturday night, at eight o'clock—he carried the plough under his arm—I said, "Have you finished your work?"—he said, "No"—I left him, and did not see him again till Monday morning—I told Clark.
WILLIAM POTTER ( policeman, K 273). I went with Clark to the prisoner, and asked if he took away any tools, on Saturday night, from the shop—he said, "No"—I said, "You were met with some"—he denied carrying any away.
JOSEPH BENTON ( policeman, K 381). I went with Potter to the prisoner's lodging, at Maryland Point, and found this plough, saw, and some tools claimed by Philip Wilson—the prisoner told me where I should find the tools.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18481023-2420]
2420. JAMES SPACKMAN and MARK BARKER , stealing 7 quarters of wheat, value 18 l.; the goods of William James Reynolds, the master of Sparkman.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM JAMES REYNOLDS . I was a farmer, at Stratford—I now live at 129, Bishopsgate-street. When I was in the farming business, Spackman was my foreman, and lived in a cottage adjoining the stables and barn, on my farm—I did not know Barker then—he keeps a horse and cart, and a little greengrocer's-shop, at Stratford—I had repeatedly spoken to Spackman about missing things—on 30th Aug. they began to reap some wheat—I reckoned, as nearly as possible, that there would be very nearly forty quarters in that threshing—this wheat was different from any other in the neighbourhood—Spackman had the charge of the barn altogether, before and after the threshing; it is quite impossible that it could have been interfered with without his knowledge, his cottage was close to the barn, and no one could come to it without his seeing them—he brought the keys in every night, and came to my window between four and five o'clock every morning, and I gave them out to him—he rendered me an account of twenty-eight quarters and five bushels—I have been a farmer ever since I was fourteen years old—I am able to calculate what quantity should be produced—the nature of the crop and the hours employed in threshing would be very fair data for the amount—on the morning of 20th Sept. I went up to Barker—I said, "Is your name Barker?"—he said, "Yes"—I said, "What did you do with the wheat that you carted from my house?"—he said, "I do not know you; I have not carted any wheat"—I said, "You carted two quarters and a half, on the 5th and on the 6th"—he said, "You know all about it; I carted, altogether, seven and a half and six bushels; I was employed by Spackman to do it"—I said, "Who?"—he said, "Spackman, Green, and Oliver"—they were men
in my employ, and were completely under Spackman's direction, and no one else—mine was white wheat, a little discoloured—when Spackman was in charge, he said, "Master, I hope you will have mercy on me in this case."
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had not he to attend to the live stock on the farm? A. Not at this period—when the threshing began I distinctly told him to attend to that only—there was no live stock at that time—I had no horse to exercise within the last ten months—there were some sheep belonging to Mr. Lowe, his man looked after them; Spackman had nothing to do with them—Hickman and Green, who worked with Spackman and Oliver on my farm, have absconded—Spackman has a wife and six children.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Have not farmers complained of very great want of the fufilment of their expectations, both in quantity and quality? A. Yes; but I put the fields down at less than half that they have yielded other years.
THOMAS ADDINGTON ( policeman, K 189). I took Barker on 20th Sept, at bis house—Mr. Reynolds gave him in charge for stealing wheat—he said, "I had nothing to do with stealing it; I own I carted it; I was employed by Spackman; he paid me 5 s. for it, and knowing he was Mr. Reynolds' foreman, I did it"—I took it to Mr. Cook's mill, in Manor-house-lane.
GEORGE ROBERTS . I live in James's-place, Stratford. I saw Barker on 6th Sept, in Stratford, with his horse and cart, with sacks in it, which appeared to be full, coming in a direction from Mr. Reynolds'—I got to Mr. Reynold's in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour afterwards, and Spackman was in the stable—next morning I met Barker again, in Angel-lane, with his horse and cart, and five or six sacks, similar to what he had the day before.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Did you count the sacks? A. No—I have known Barker twelve months—I never went to his house—I knew he had a horse and cart—I never knew him to let them out.
MARIA ROBERTS . I am the wife of George Roberts. About a quarter-past five o'clock, on 8th Sept., I was going to work—Barker passed me in his cart—he seemed to have some sacks or a sack in it—I cannot tell whether full or empty—the mouth hung over the cart—he went straight towards Mr. Reynolds's farm, towards the potato-shed, which is close to the barn—I cannot tell what he did—I saw him coming away, and Spackman passed by the end of the stable towards his house—Barker was back at about half-past five—I said, "Good morning, Mr. Barker"—he gave me no answer.
WILLIAM BARNARD . I am in the employ of Mr. Cook, a miller, at Stratford. On 6th Sept. Barker brought five sacks of wheat, five on the 7th, and five on the 8th—he did not say on whose account he brought them—the wheat was shot and ground—it was nearly all white wheat, but discoloured—it was mixed with some more corn in a bin to be ground.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Do you purchase large quantities of wheat? A. Sometimes—we purchase wheat of various growths, some white and red, and some discoloured.
WILLIAM BONNICK . I superintend Mr. Cook's mill—Barker Came to me on the evening of 5th Sept., and asked if I was a buyer of wheat—he brought a sample, and said there was about seven quarters and six bushels—I agreed to buy five quarters of him—he showed me this paper, "15 sacks 2 bushels," to show the handwriting where it came from—it is dated Harlow, in Essex—I gave 54 s. a quarter for the wheat—on the 6th he brought five sacks, on the 7th five, and on the 8th two quarters and six bushels, and that I could not agree for—I did not purchase it then—I bought it on the following Wednesday
—the markets were declining—I knew the wheat that Mr. Reynolds grew; it was white wheat—I had dealt with Barker before for wheat—he said this wheat came from his brother-in-law—he had told me before that his brother-in-law lived at Hailow, in Essex—he had the sacks back again on the 7th.
WILLIAM POTTER ( police-man. K 273). I searched Barker's house, and found six empty sacks, with "W. Cook" on them—I asked Barker how he came by them; he said they were sacks he had borrowed of Mr. Cook to fetch some wheat away from Mr. Reynolds farm, and he had 5 s. a load for carting them—I sent word to Mr. Cook, and he said they were his sacks, and he had lent them to Barker.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you take Spackman? A. Yes—I said I took him for some potatoes and corn—he said, "I expected so, but I am innocent."
WILLIAM YORK . I am foreman to Mr. Rees, a timber-merchant, at Stratford. I know Barker—he said he had some wheat coming from his brother-in-law, and requested me to write this paper for him—I did so.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. When was this? A. I cannot say—I believe the date is on it—it might have been four or five months ago.
(Daniel Hayes, a draper of Stepney, and Mr. Seagrave, a joiner, gave Barker a good character).
SPACKMAN GUILTY . Aged 33.
BARKER GUILTY . Aged 42.
Transported for Seven ears .
[trialAccount t18481023-2421]

GUILTY . Aged 42.— Confined Six Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18481023-2422]
2422. JOHN ANGWORTH and SARAH ANGWORTH , stealing part of a machine, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Henry Bewley and others; John having been before convicted.
FREDERICK PALMER MOORE . I am manager of the Gutta Percha Works at Stratford. This iron belongs to the Company, Henry Bewley and others—it was in their custody on their premises—I saw it safe at the stock-taking on 29th or 30th June—the prisoner John was employed by the party of whom we rent the factory—he was often on the premises—Sarah was always at her house, which is part of the factory—I understand they are man and wile.
Cross-examined by MR. HUGHES. Q. How do you identify it? A. By its fitting a machine; it is a very old machine, and never used, to my knowledge—it is worth 3 s.
JOSEPH BENTON ( policeman). On 6th Oct., about seven o'clock, I was on duty at Stratford, and saw the prisoners—John had a sack under his arm, with something very heavy in it—I watched them to a marine store dealers—he gave it to Sarah at the door—she took it in—he went on a little—I went into the shop—the man would not buy it—I spoke to Sarah—she did not answer me—I told the man to detain her, went after John, and brought him back to the shop—he said it was his; that he took it out of a ditch, and had had it twelve months—then he said he took it out of some dirt in the yard, and had had it three months—he took me by the collar, while Sarah escaped—Potter came to my assistance—we took him to the station—he there said he had had it six months—I went to the works—it fitted the screw-holes—they had lately been torn out.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the machine in the yard? A. Yes; a ditch runs close by—it seemed all to pieces—another part of it was gone.
WILLIAM POTTER ( policeman, K 273). I went to Benton's assistance, and took John—he said he had dug the iron out of a heap of dirt; that he had dug barrows-full out before, and sold it at the same shop—it exactly fits one side of the machine.
WILLIAM WARD . I am a marine-store dealer at Stratford. I bought a bar of iron of Sarah, between three and four o'clock in the evening before.
CHARLES WILLEY . I have been in the service of the Gutta Percha Company—this iron belongs to a machine used by them.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you ever seen it whole? A. Yes; I used it twelve months ago.
HENRY DARBY ( policeman). I produce a certificate of the prisoner John's conviction—( read—Convicted, Dec. 1845— confined six months).—I was present—he is the person.
SARAH — NOT GUILTY .
JOHN — GUILTY . Aged 34.— Transported for Seven Years .

2424. HENRY FIELD , stealing one handkerchief, value 4 s.; the goods of William Maynard; having been before convicted.

MARY REED . I was at Mr. Maynard's, and saw the prisoner take a handkerchief from the line, and walk away with it—I knew him by sight about Bow—I told Mrs. Maynard.
Prisoner's Defence. I was not in Stratford that morning.
CHARLES ROWBURY ( policeman). I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction—( read—Convicted Jan. 1848— confined six months)—I was present—he is the person.

2430. CATHERINE DALY , stealing 7lbs., weight of potatoes, value 6 d.; the goods of Hill Dodds, her master.

JOSEPH GREEN ( policeman, R 315). On the 25th Sept. I was on duty in Lee-road, and I saw the prisoner—I asked her what she had on her—she said, "Nothing"—I found on her 71bs. of potatoes—she said her master had given her leave to take them—at the station she said she stole them, and they were Mr. Dodd's property.

2431. EMMA GARTHWAITE , stealing 14 handkerchiefs, and other goods, value 2 l. 7 s. 6 d.; the goods of Richard Shillingford and another; to which she pleaded

Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18481023-2432]
2432. ELIZA STACEY , stealing 1 watch, value 15 l.; 1 guard-chain, and other articles, 15 l.; the goods of James Suter, in the dwelling-house of Mary Jones.
HENRY GILLIES ( policeman, R 327). On Monday, 2nd Oct., at two o'clock in the morning, I was on duty in Rose-street, Greenwich—I heard a cry of "Murder!"—I went to the place whence it proceeded, knocked at the door, and the moment I got in I found the keeper of the house and the prisoner struggling—the prisoner had this watch in her hand—the woman charged her with stealing it—I thought the prisoner was endeavouring to hide it amongst the bed-clothes—I pinioned her arms and took it from her—I asked Mr. Suter, who was there, if he had lost anything else—he said, "Yes; a ring"—I then pinioned her arms again, and this ring fell from her hand—an officer who was with me took some money out of her hand—the prisoner made use of bad language, and said we were sharks —she afterwards said she was going to take care of it, and give it to the gentleman—she was dressed, except her stays, which were under her arm.
Prisoner. He took the watch out of the gentleman's hand, across the bed. Witness. No; the watch was in your hand.
JANE JONES . I live at Greenwich, and keep a lodging-house for casual visitors of both sexes—the prisoner came to my house on Sunday evening, 1st Oct., about half-past eleven o'clock, with Suter—he was very much in liquor—she asked him if he would give her a little to drink—he said, "Yes"—he took half-a-crown out of his pocket, and gave it to me, to fetch half-a-pint of gin—I gave him the 2 s. change, and he gave it me, to pay for the bed—I went a second time for gin for him, and he gave me a sovereign to get it—between one and two 2 heard the prisoner walking about in the room—I went to the street-door—the prisoner came into the passage, and said she was going to get a pot of ale, as she made it a rule to have a pot of ale on the table, as she was thirsty in the night—I said, "You are not going out; I will fetch it," and I sent a boy for it—I had noticed the prosecutor's watch and chain on the drawers—I afterwards saw them in the prisoner's hand—when I went into the room with the pot of ale, she endeavoured to keep me out; but I would go in, and the gentleman's clothes were on the floor, and his watch was in her hand—I took hold of her, and desired hex to put the watch down—she said she would not—she said he had little or no money, and she would not stop with any man, unless he had 3 l. or 4 l.—I struck her, to try to get the watch from her—she cried "Murder!" and the officers came to the door.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not take part of the beer, and were you not in the room? A. No—I did not tell you to ply him with plenty of gin—you said you would not take a farthing less than 3 l. or 4 l., and if you had been anywhere else, you should have taken it before—I did not tell you to go out of the house after you had been to bed with him and got up again.
Prisoner. It was your intention to rob him, not mine; you fetched four half-pints of gin; I was not over the threshold of the door; if the gentleman had been sober, I should not have been here now; you will swear anything.
JAMES SUTER . I live at the Grove, Blackheath, and am clerk to my father. On 1st Oct. I came to London, and drank freely with some friends whom I met—I reached Greenwich about eleven o'clock at night—I believe I met the prisoner, and went home with her—I do not recollect her person—I was in that state that I do not recollect anything—it might have been the prisoner—I was awoke by the policemen the next morning and missed this
ring from my finger, some silver from my pocket, and this watch—it is mine—I do not recollect that I gave it to anybody—I am sure I did not—I have no recollection of what passed.
Prisoner's Defence. I had no intention of robbing him; she wanted to make me go out of the house after the gentleman was in bed and asleep; I had been to bed and got up again, because the gentleman was taken sick; I went to get the basin for him.
JURY to MRS. JONES Q. Where was the prisoner when you saw her? A. She came out of the bed-room into the passage next to the street-door—she had left the bed-room, and she had the property in her right hand—I pushed her back into the bed-room, with it.
GUILTY. Aged 35.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor. — Transported for Seven Years.

SOPHIA TAYLOR . About half-past six o'clock on 28th Sept. the two prisoners came to me, and offered this boot for sale—I refused to buy it—Murphy used very bad language, and took up a knife, and made several blows at me—I sent for an officer.

Murphy. I was in a state of intoxication.
Donoghley. I was not with him.

[trialAccount t18481023-2440]
2440. ELIJAH REED , stealing 1 watch, value 7 l. 10 s.; 1 half-sovereign, 1 shilling, 1 sixpence, 2 pence, and 1 halfpenny; the property of George Cotterell, from his person.
GEORGE COTTERELL . I live at 5, Little Barlow-street, Marylebone. On 19th Sept. I was at the Lord Duncan, at Deptford, between twelve and one o'clock at night—the prisoner was at the door as I entered—he had a cab there, and proposed to take me in it to London-bridge for 1 s.; but after we got outside the door I asked him what part of London he was going to—he asked where I wanted to go to, and I said if I stopped by Bond-street I should be near home—he said he was going there, and would take me for 18 d.—I said if he would I would give him a drop of beer—he required payment from me at the public-house, which I refused—he said he had taken fares frequently, and when they got to their destination they had not money to pay with—I asked him if he thought it was the same with me—he said, "Yes," and I showed him half a sovereign, and some silver, and returned it to my pocket—he said, "Old fellow, I may as well have your shilling as any one else's"—he had some beer and tobacco, which I paid for—I was going to get on the box by him—he said I had better get inside; would I have no objection to his looking for another fare—I said, "No," and got inside—he put up both the windows—I objected, and he said, "When you get to your destination, if you should be asleep, I will wake you up, and he drove on—the cab stopped in the Westminster-road—I woke up directly; I was not sound asleep—I found it was not my destination, and slumbered again—about two minutes after the prisoner came back with some other parties, two, I think, and said, "I have got a b----y old bloke here," or words to that effect—I do not know what that means; he was speaking to the other parties—I distinctly heard the words—there was some conversation—the door then opened, and he said, "He is sound asleep"—I felt a handkerchief vibrating in the air several times, and I was completely insensible, and yet conscious of what was going forward—a hand was afterwards put into my waistcoat-pocket twice—I heard a party say, "Put his knife back again;" that was returned, and I felt a hand in my trowser's-pocket, and I rose up in the stupified state, in which I was, and turned round, inclining on my right side, and said, "What are you doing?'"—there was again a vibration of a handkerchief seven or eight times, and I felt a hand at my trowser's pocket, and in about two minutes the prisoner came round to the door, and said, "Old fellow, I can't take you any further for nothing"—he took me out with violence on the pavement, and drove away—I ran after the cab about 150 or 200 yards, when I was tripped up, and the first person I saw after I got up again was a policeman, to whom I related the circumstance—I went to the station-house, and gave information, and went the following morning to the Badge-office—I got information the following day, and he was taken on the Saturday following—I lost 1 s., 6 d., a half-sovereign, my watch, and 2 1/2 d., or 3 d.—I know I had it about me when I awoke in the Westminster-road, and it was all gone when I was pulled out of the cab —I cannot tell what they gave me to inhale,
but I felt completely stupified; I felt a vibration in the air to and fro—I had had nothing to drink with him—I had walked from Woolwich, having lost the conveyance—I am perfectly satisfied the prisoner is the man that drove me from Deptford to the Westminster-road.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALFE. Q. Have you always been satisfied he is the man? A. Quite; I never mistook any other man—I never asked any other person whether he was the person that drove me—I asked a man named White whether he was down at Deptford on the Tuesday night—he said he was—I did not ask him whether he brought me from Deptford; I positively swear that—I asked him if he had brought a fare from Deptford—I asked that to apprehend the prisoner, because he was in conversation with him at Deptford half an hour before I entered the house—I had no doubt he would say he knew nothing about it—the prisoner showed me his badge at Deptford—I was not satisfied whether it was 2696, or 2969: but it was four numbers, and I know the left-hand figure was two—I went to the Badge-office, and found such a badge was not licensed—I then went to the station-house again—they could give me no assistance or information—I then went to the Lord Duncan, at Deptford—they did not know the cabman—directly I saw the prisoner I recognized him, and he said, "That is the man that I 1 brought from Deptford," speaking to me—I had been at work that day from five to one o'clock—I then went from London-bridge to Greenwich, where I transacted some business with an ironmonger—after that, it being fine, I went to Woolwich, and remained there till I lost the last boat to London—I was drinking and enjoying myself at the Waterman—the last steamer started I about a quarter to eight—it was a quarter to one when I started with the prisoner from Deptford—I stopped at Woolwich, after my brother left of ork with his shipmates, till twenty past ten—I then walked through Green-wich to Deptford, and met the prisoner—I did not drink anything after I left Woolwich; I drank nothing at Deptford—I accepted the glass out of the prisoner's hand, and just put it to my lips, but did not drink anything—I had not been in any other house before that—it was the first time I was at the Lord Duncan—I do not know whether I came up the Old Kent-road—I do not know my road about that way—I cannot say whether I car through a toll-bar; I believe there is a toll-bar—I do not know whether it is customary to pay a fresh toll after twelve—I did not pay toll; I swear I did not pay any money after leaving the public-house—I did not pay the prisoner any money before leaving the public-house—I was to pay him 18 d., and he was to have a glass of anything he liked to drink, when we got to London—he had a pint of porter, half a quartern of gin, and a pipe of tobacco, before we started, at my expense—it might have been twenty minutes after leaving Deptford that I went to sleep—it might have taken an hour or an hour and a quarter altogether coming from Deptford—I found myself in the Westminster-road about a quarter to two o'clock—I was not sound asleep the whole time, I did not doze the whole time—I tried to amuse myself by looking through the glass, perhaps about a quarter of an hour, and then 1 dozed, and after that I have very little recollection of the objects along the road—I have not the slightest recollection of the toll-bar—I am certain the toll-man did not put his head into the window, and I pay him the toll—we first stopped in the road in a line with Westminster-bridge, very near a night-house there—I know it was in a line with west-minster-bridge, because I went over the bridge afterwards—I do not know the neighbourhood much—I did not know where I was when I first looked out of the cab window when it stopped; I did not know till afterward—I saw
it was not my destination—I was not very sleepy—I thought the prisoner had got off for his own convenience, and I dozed off again—I leaned back—I did not see the waterman there when I was pulled out—I did not stop to see any person—I might have seen him, but did not recognise him—I followed the cab —I am certain the cab did not go on again after I looked out of the window, previous to my being thrown out of the cab —I was not asleep, though dozing—I will undertake positively to swear the cab did not go on after I looked out of the window till I was pulled out—I have no recollection of the caiman stopping and taking up another fare—I do not recollect a woman being in the cab —I heard a woman's voice outside the cab door when it stopped—no person got into the cab after I left Deptford till I was pulled out—a woman and a man did not get in—I am quite sure I was not at all sick during the evening—I had drunk porter and a glass of gin and water, no brandy, nor rum, or wine, or ale, only porter and gin—the jolting of the cab did not make me sick—I am quite positive I was not sick in or out of the cab.
COURT. Q. How long were you under the influence of what you suppose was a stupifying medicine? A. Three hours after I was taken to the station I was not in my original senses; I felt, in fact, completely stupified—while it was strong in operation I felt what occurred to me, but I could not move to resist it—I distinctly felt my pockets rifled—the prisoner said, "I have got a b----old bloke here," speaking to the parties he had about the cab door.
ROBERT JONES ( policeman). On Tuesday night, 19th Sept., I was on duty in the Westminster-road, and at a little before two o'clock saw the prisoner pass me, driving a cab —I had an opportunity of seeing him, his face was towards me—after he had got fifteen or twenty yards he commenced flogging his horse, put it into a gallop, and went down York-road—I saw Cotterell running after the cab along the Westminster-road—he gave an alarm, and told me he had been robbed of his watch, a half-sovereign, 1 s. 6 d., and some copper—the cab was going very fast down the York-road—the prosecutor was quite sober—he was excited, but not from drink—he went with me to the station—I found the prisoner in custody on the following Thursday, and knew him again.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you seen him before? A. Yes, on different ranks—I was standing under a lamp as he passed me—it was in the bend of the road—it is rather broad—it was at two o'clock in the morning—he did not go very fast till after he had passed me ten or fifteen yards—he turned and looked at me, and the gas shone full in his face—after he passed me he began to whip his horse, and if I had caught the number I should have taken it, for furious driving—that was why I took notice of him—it was about fifty yards from where the prosecutor was, and for that fifty yards, till he passed me, he was cantering his horse, going at the ordinary pace—I saw both the prisoner and the prosecutor before he came up to me—the prosecutor did not give any alarm till he came up to the corner of York-road—he was excited, but not drunk—I did not see him roll over—I cannot say whether there were other people there then, I was not there—two females came up from Westminster-bridge way—there might or there might not have been people coming in the same direction as the prosecutor—I did not notice anybody—the prosecutor said he had been thrown on the pavement—he said he had either fallen down, or he believed he had been tripped up by one of the prisoner's associates, as he was pursuing the cab.
JAMES FROST ( policeman.) I apprehended the prisoner on Saturday, 25th
Sept., in York-street, York-road—I told him I wanted him for robbing a person in his cab —I hesitated a little, as his wife was there, and told him I would tell him what it was for as we went to the station—he requested me to tell him, and I told him it was for robbing a man of his watch, whom he brought from Deptford in his cab —he said he had brought an old bloke from Deptford, and as he was conveying him to the Westminster-road he took up another fare, and as the fare was in the cab he was sick all over the other parties, and they got fighting in the cab together; that he put the other fare down at the Elephant and Castle, and brought him into the Westminster-road, and thought he had brought him far enough for 1 s., and there he bundled him out of the cab, and drove off—as we were going to the Police-court, he said, "I can see the old bloke means to stick to me, I can see it in his ways," or words to that effect—the word " bloke " is a common term used among cabmen —I cannot say the meaning of it.
Cross-examined. Q. When you went to him he at once admitted he had brought a fare from Deptford? A. He did—I believe there is a toll-bar between Deptford and the Westminster-road.
ELIZABETH HAY . I am wife of William Hay, who keeps the Lord Duncan public-house, at Deptford. I recollect the prisoner being in that house on a Tuesday, a fortnight before I was examined—the prosecutor was there, and treated him to half a quartern of gin, a pint of porter, and a pipe of tobacco—I did not see the prosecutor take anything—I did not perceive that he was at all intoxicated—he did not give the prisoner any money in front of the bar.
Cross-examined. Q. He paid for what the prisoner had? A. Yes—I believe the prisoner drank it all—part of the gin was left on the counter, the prisoner drank the rest—I did not see the prosecutor put the glass to his lips—I believe I should have seen him if he had—I was close to him—he did not appear to have been drinking at all when he came in—he seemed a little fatigued—he said he had walked from Woolwich—he came in between twelve and one o'clock, and remained about a quarter of an hour—I saw him and the prisoner start together—the prisoner appeared perfectly sober.
GEORGE HEWETT . I am waterman, at Lambeth-place, Lambeth-square. The prisoner drove up there on the morning of 20th Sept.—I saw him open the cab-door, and the prosecutor came out very quickly, and fell on the pavement as he got out—in about two minutes the prisoner got on the box again and drove on very steady, and when he got his horse straight he shoved on, but at a very moderate pace—I did not see him five yards—he did not start at a quick pace.
Cross-examined. Q. He kept a moderate pace during that time? A. Yes—I saw the prisoner open the cab-door, and saw the prosecutor fall on tie pavement that moment—he came out very quickly at the door—I cannot say whether he fell out, or whether he was pulled out—I was very busy at the time—I had three horses in front, and happened to cast my eye that way when the door opened—it was a very short time after the cab drew up, hardly two minutes—the man jumped down off his box and opened the door directly, and the man tumbled out on the pavement.
JURY. Q. Did you see anybody about the cab at the time the man fell out? A. No—the stand is in Mount-street, Westminster-road—the cab stopped on the right side of the watering-place as you come down the New Cut—it is in a straight line with Westminster-bridge.
GEORGE COTTERFLL re-examined. I did not pay my fare—it was not asked for—I was pulled at of the cab with a great deal of violence, and the
prisoner directly got on the box and drove off—I did not see any one else about the cab when I got up—my watch has not been traced.
(John Alien, tobacconist, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Transported for Ten Years .

2441. EDWIN HOUGHTON , forging and uttering a request for the delivery of goods, with intent to defraud James Grey; to which he pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Justice Maule.

2442. SPENCER LINDFIELD, MARY ANN DRYDEN , and RICHARD ORPIN were indicted for the wilful murder of Eliza Wilson, by procuring her miscarriage with the use of a catheter; they were also charged, on the Coroner's inquisition with a like offence.
MESSRS. RYLAND and LAURIE conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL ELYARD, ESQ . I am one of Her Majesty's Justices of the Peace for the county of Surrey. On Wednesday, 20th Sept., the deceased, Eliza Wilson, made a statement in my presence and hearing, and signed it—I took it down, but in consequence of her weak, dying state, I had to bend over her in bed, and then I got the police-inspector to write part, in my presence—I had seen her on the previous Sunday—I was requested by the inspector to go there on that day to take the declaration of a dying female—she was then very ill, but she expected to recover, and I therefore went away—on the Wednesday the inspector came again to me, and I went again—I saw her father, and sent for a Clergyman—I asked her how she found herself—she said, "Very ill"—I said, "Do you now think you will recover?"—she said, "No"—I said, "Do you think you will die?"—she said, "Yes"—I then proceeded to take the statement.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. She was lying in her bed at the time, I suppose? A. Yes—the Clergyman, the inspector, the nurse, and myself were in the room—I only addressed to her those observations I have stated—I asked her age—she appeared clearly to understand what she stated to me, though she was extremely weak; and after each sentence the nurse put some moisture to her lips—no one had to repeat what she said—she related the whole herself.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. Did you send for the Clergyman? A. Yes—before I went into the room I had not sent him to her to confer with, as to whether she would recover or not—she wrote her name with very great difficulty, indeed—I believe she died at half-past three o'clock on the Friday morning—it might have been eleven or twelve on the Wednesday that she made the statement.
ROBERT EMERSON ( police-inspector). I went with Mr. Elyard to Mr. Wilson's house at Norwood, on 20th Sept., and saw the deceased Eliza Wilson—I heard her make a statement—I wrote part of it down—this is it—she signed it—( read —" The declaration of Eliza Wilson, taken before me, Samuel Elyard, Esq., at Norwood, 20th Sept., 1848. The said Eliza Wilson having been examined by me as to whether she at present considers herself in a dying state, and she having assured me that she does, is in the thirty-second year of her age, and saith, and suspecting herself to be in the family-way, she made known her suspicion to Richard Orpin, with whom I had had criminal connection, and who would have been the father of the child—I never had connection with any other man—and on my informing him as above, he said, 'Stop till Monday, and we will go together to East-lane, Walworth, to a
woman, but she need not trouble herself for three or four months;' and he went with me on Monday, 4th Sept., to East-lane, but could not find the woman, and on the following day, Tuesday, I went by myself and did not find her, but saw a Mrs. Dryden, who resides in York-street, Walworth, to whom I acquainted that I was in the family-way, and informed her that I wished to get rid of it, and she said it should be all right, if I would pay her 4 s., and which I gave her; she gave me a box of pills and a bottle of stuff, which did not have the expected effect. Mrs. Dryden then went with me on the Wednesday to Mrs. Lindfield, to Prior-place, East-street, Walworth, a herb shop. Mrs. Dryden said, 'I have got a friend of mine come to see you.' Mrs. Lindfield, without asking any questions, said, 'I will take her up-stairs directly,' and shortly after did so, and told me to lay on the bed on my left side; she then performed an operation by some instrument, which had some holes at the end. I went again on the 9th, she used the instrument again, saying, 'It does not come so freely from you.' I slept at Mrs. Dryden's that night. I paid her, Mrs. Lindfield, 2 l. 10 s., the first day I went, and gave Mrs. Dryden a few shillings for her trouble in taking me to Mrs. Lindfield. I went to Mrs. Lindfield again on the following Monday, the 11th, when she again used the instrument, when I came home and became ill. I told Mrs. Lindfield my father thought I was ill, and she said, 'Nonsense, you are all right.' Richard Orpin promised to pay the 2 l. 10 s., but has not done so. The young man, called Mrs. Lindfield's son, was present on each occasion I went, and was perfectly aware of the business which I was on")—the prisoners were in custody before I saw Wilson—Lindfield's house and premises were searched in my presence—I was present when the catheter, a box containing other instruments, a quantity of herbs and pills, and drugs of almost all sorts, were found—Merritt, the policeman who was present, has them.
JAMES BENSON WILSON . I am the father of the deceased; her name was Eliza—she lived with me at Norwood—she was single, and nearly thirty-two years old—she was ill, and died early in the morning of 22d Sept.—I saw her after death—I had seen her alive on the Thursday night—she first became ill on 12th Sept.—previous to that she had tolerably good health—about a week before she was taken ill she went out, and was out one night and part of two days—I do not know where she went—when she became ill, Mr. Chapman, a surgeon, was sent for, and he attended her till her death.
JAMES WILSON . I am brother of deceased and son of the last witness, and live with him at Norwood. On Thursday, 14th Sept., I took a note from my sister, which she wrote to Mrs. Lindfield, Prior-place, East-street, Walworth—the name of Lindfield was on the door—I got there about eleven o'clock at night—I rung twice, and I believe her son came to the door—I inquired for Mrs. Lindfield, and put the note under the door—he went up-stairs, came down again, and I was then admitted, and taken up-stairs into the front room, where Mrs. Lindfield was in bed—she had the note that 1 had taken in her hand—she said, "You come from Norwood?"—I said, "Yes"—she said, "She complains of pain?"—I said yes, she did; she was very bad—I meant my sister—I said she was very bad, and I expected the doctor when I got back—she gave me two ounces of Epsom salts, which I was to give to my sister, and she was to take them in some warm water, and she was to have plenty of warm drink, and likewise to sit on hot water—I asked her what was the matter with my sister—she made no answer, only said she complained of pain—I asked her again—she said that she had the liver complaint, but she was a strong constitution, and she would soon
get over it—I asked how many times my sister had been there—she said twice, once in the week before, and on the Monday—" Epsom Salts" was written on the paper she gave me—she did not say when or how often my sister was to take them, I was to give them to her when I got back—as I was coming away she said I had no occasion to say that she had asked me any questions—I took the salts to my sister, and she took part of them.
WILLIAM WOODWARD ( policeman, B 157). On Sunday, 17th Sept., about eight o'clock in the evening, I went to Lindfield's house, 24, Prior-place, East-street—I was in plain clothes—I saw a young man there, named William Lindfield—I asked to see Mrs. Lindfield in private, and he took me into a parlour adjoining the shop—I said I came from a young woman, Eliza Wilson, living at Norwood—she asked how she was—I said she was very bad, she was in a feverish state—she asked whether she had taken the medicine she had sent by her brother, and put her feet in warm water—I said I did Dot exactly know—I asked her who brought the young woman to her house, and she said a person living in York-street, who sold oysters—I asked her if her name was Dryden—she said she did not know, she had not seen her above twice—she said the person that brought her had given her some pills and a bottle of savin water, which had caused a stoppage in the bowels and the water—I told her I would call again in the morning, and let her know how the young woman was—I then went away—I called again the next morning, about ten, I was shown into the parlour—she asked me how the young woman was—I said, "She is very bad; you are not aware who I am?"—she said, "No"—I said, "I am a police-constable; consider yourself in charge for using a certain instrument on Eliza Wilson, and procuring abortion, and thereby endangering her life"—she then said, "The only instrument I used on her was to draw off the water, and 1 can't recollect whether it was three or four times," she produced this instrument ( produced)—I then asked her if she had received any money from the young woman—she said she had had 50 s.; 8 s. 6 d. she had given the woman that brought her, 2 s. she charged for medicine, and she had returned Eliza Wilson two sovereigns—this letter was produced by her to the Magistrate, and it has been in my possession ever since ( produced).
JAMES WILSON re-examined. This is the letter I carried from my sister to Mrs. Lindfield—( read —"Norwood, Sept. 14th, 1848.—Mrs. Lindfield, I have sent my brother to inform you I am so bad, I cannot since Monday night move any of my limbs. I have been unwell a little, but nothing else whatever. Pray do not let them into the secret yet; they are very angry because I will not have a doctor. I feel so bad, I think I shall not live, pray send me word what 1 had better do. I am so very much swelled nothing goes through me. I have taken two doses of castor oil, have the goodness to send me word what had better be done. I have got such a dreadful pain in my left side: my dear Mrs. Lindfield, do send me some ease. E. Wilson.")
THOMAS CANNON ( policeman, P 59). I took Dryden into custody on Monday, 18th Sept., in York-street, Lock's-fields, Walworth, about half a mile from East-lane. I told her I was a constable, that Lindfield was in custody, and she was also charged with her with endangering the life of Eliza Wilson, by trying means to procure abortion—she said, "It is through nothing that I have given her, I only gave her some five-leaved grass, ground ivy, and pil. cochice pills; I saw her twice on Monday, and last Monday week"—I asked her if she had received any money from Mrs. Lindfield—she said she had received none, and knew nothing of Mrs. Lindfield, but merely took the female there to
show her the house—she afterwards said she had received 8 s. from Mrs. Lindheld, and 3 s. from Eliza Wilson for medicine—I then took her into custody—on the following day I apprehended Orpin at Rose Cottage, Chapel road, Norwood—I told him I was a constable, and he was charged with two females then in custody with endangering the life of Eliza Wilson, by persuading her to take steps to procure abortion—he said, "I know nothing about is"—his wife came into the room, and said, "I do not know whether he is Guilty or not, but I have challenged him with it"—he said, "You had no business to have had her in the house"—the wife said she should not have taken any stuff, and then there would have been nothing the matter with her—Orpin then said, "What stuff she has taken has nothing to do with me"—he asked me who charged him—I said, "I should for the present"—he said, "Whatever she has done is of her own accord; if she takes stuff to poison herself, it is not my fault"—I went to Lindfield's house on the evening of 19th, searched it, and found this box of instruments ( produced), also a bill of 6 l. 10 s. 8 d. paid by Lindfield to Dryden, which I found in a desk at the end of the counter—she was then in custody—it is a herb shop—her son William lived in the house—I also found a list of herbs.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was Lindfield's son in custody? A. No—he said he had some bills in the desk—I do not know whether he claimed this one—I do not know whether he claimed the whole of the contents of the desk.
WILLIAM MERRITT ( policeman). I assisted in searching Lindfield's house—I found a quantity of herbs of which I produce samples—there is some savin—I also searched Dryden's house, and found herbs there, and a small portion of savin—I only know savin by its being pointed out to me as such—I found herbs of different kinds at both places—at Dryden's I found these two bills, and two cards. ( These were headed, "Mrs. Lindfield, Midwife, Dentist, and Practical Herbalist, &c," and contained a long list of herbs, ointments, &c., for the cure of various disorders; and the words' retirement for ailing females. '")
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was this likewise found at Lindfield's? A. Yes—( read —"This is to certify that Mrs. Spencer Lind-field has diligently attended a course of my lectures on midwifery, and the diseases of women and children; and practised the same under my inspection.—24th May, 1821.—Henry Gore Clough, M. D., Accoucheur and Physician (to several institutions), Professor and Lecturer on the Science of Midwifery, Berners-street")—these herbs, and a variety of other articles were shown to Dr. Flower, and I believe he examined the whole of them by the direction of the Magistrate—I believe he gave evidence—the desk found at Lindfield's was claimed by her son.
JOSEPH ARMSTRONG ( policeman). I have been stationed at Norwood for the last four years—I have known the deceased, Eliza Wilson, and Orpin about four years—they both lived at Norwood—I have seen then together several times; at all hours, between dark, and until half-past one o'clock in the morning, down Thurlow-place, and other parts of the village—I have seen Orpin kiss her.
JOHN CHAPMAN . I am a surgeon at Thurlow-place, Norwood. On 14th Sept., I was called to attend the deceased—I had not known her before—I did not see her till the morning of the 15th—I found her eriously ill. suffering from great constitutional disturbance, and apparently in a very dangerous state—she appeared to be in pain at the lower part of the abdomen—she constantly complained of sickness, but did not vomit while I was there—I
attended her till her death—I consulted with a physician about her case on 16th Sept., and treated her accordingly—in consequence of the complaints she made, I thought it necessary to examine her person—I did that the very first time I saw her—I found indications of a miscarriage taking place—about thirty-six hours after death I made an examination of the body, assisted by Mr. Ray—she had certainly been pregnant, and was in my judgment seven weeks advanced—( The particulars of the post-mortem examination—and of the cross-examination of this witness, were not of a nature fit for publication; he attributed death to inflammation of the uterus, arising from abortion, but stated that the injury which appeared to have produced that inflammation was not occasioned by the use of the catheter.)
EDWARD RAY . I am a surgeon, of Dulwich. I assisted Mr. Chapman in the post-mortem examination—I attribute death to the combined effects of inflammation of the bladder, the uterus, and the peritoneum—she had certainly advanced into the second month of pregnancy—abortion had taken place, and I should suppose from unnatural causes.
Cross-examined. Q. It constantly arises from natural causes, does it not? A. It is not common, unless violence is employed—all the appearances I found might have arisen from natural causes if there was severe inflammation—I have not known severe inflammation supervene on abortion—I have read of such cases, even when produced by what I should call natural causes.
ROBERT EMERSON re-examined. I was present at the examinations of the prisoners before the Magistrate—they were asked if they chose to say anything, after the evidence had been taken on both examinations—a statement was made by Mrs. Lindfield on the first examination, and it was read over to her again on the second—she said that was what she wished to state, and I saw the Magistrate sign it—I did not see her sign it—Dryden made a statement at the first examination, which was read over to her, and I believe the Magistrate signed it—I know his signature—Orpin made a statement at the last examination—it was read over to him, and I believe the Magistrate signed it—these three signatures are Mr. Norton's—( read —"Spencer Lindfield says: On 4th Sept. this patient (the deceased) was brought to me the first time; she bad been under this woman (Dryden) for a fortnight; she did not understand midwifery, and she was brought to me; she said she had been giving her an infusion of savin; that her bowels were so bad she did not know how to bear herself; she (the deceased) said she wished me to examine her; that it was only three weeks since the. intimacy had taken place, and that there had been but one stoppage; I told her that from that period to three months it was my opinion that no midwife could say whether a woman was pregnant or not. I wish that some medical man should examine my hand, to see whether it is in a state to reach the uterus of a person in a rigid state, like this young woman's. I did what I could, but I told her I could prove nothing. I told her to keep her mind easy, and gave her some laxitive medicine. When I tr ied to examine her next time, I had not got my finger more than an inch when I found menstruation coming on again, and I told her to go to bed, and keep her feet warm. I drew her water off for her, as she complained of pain. I gave her an ounce of salts to take home, and I gave her at the same time four pills. I told her to take half an ounce of salts that night, and next day to take two pills, but no salts. I said, 'Take them alternately, till your bowels are relieved.' She complained of costive-ness very much. I did not see her again till that day week, and then she showed me appearances to satisfy me ail was right. She said she was going to see her aunt; she complained that her bowels were still in a constipated
state. I attributed it to the properties of the savin. I heard no more of her till Thursday evening again, when I received this note; then she complained only of the constipation of the bowels, and I sent her two ounces of salts by a young man who brought this note. Dryden did not say she had given her savin; it was the young woman said that, when I took her up stairs privately to examine her. It was the third time the deceased came to me I drew off her water." Dryden says, "She (deceased) had no savin water from me. I gave her rive-leaf grass, ground-ivy, agrimony, and pil. cochioe, which are fine things for the stomach. She asked me if I knew a midwife, and I referred her to Mrs. Lindfield." Orpin says, "I have been out with the young woman several times, but as to giving her poison it is wrong; whatever she took she got it, and took it of her own accord; I neither supplied her with money nor bought it myself; she said something must be done; she came to me, and asked me to give her 30 s. to get her some stuff, but 1 told her I would not have anything to do with it, and 1 have not seen her since.")
(Several females deposed to Lindfield's good character, and to her skill as a midwife.")
NOT GUILTY .
(There was another indictment against the prisoners, charging them with the like murder, by the administration of savin; and also an indictment against Dryden and Orpin for administering savin, with intent to procure miscarriage; upon which MR. RYLAND offered no evidence.
NOT GUILTY .

2443. GEORGE PENNY , for feloniously knowing and abusing Ann Warner, a girl aged 13 years.
GUILTY of an Assault. — Confined One Year .
Before Mr. Recorder.

2444. ADOLPHUS SOLITA , feloniously killing and slaying Robert Woodrow.
MR. PLATT conducted the Prosecution.
FRANCES SMITH . I live at 1, Elliott's-place, Lambeth, with my sister, who is the mother of the deceased boy, Robert Woodrow—he would have been thirteen years old next Jan.—he came home on Saturday evening, 9th Sept., from Mr. Randall's, his master, a boot and shoemaker, at Camberwell—he sat down on a chair, and complained of his head aching—I asked what made his head ache, and he said a boy had struck him at the back of his head, and he said to me, "Aunt, feel what a lump I have got?"—I felt a lump, about the size of a nut, behind his ear—I did not think it of any great importance; I thought it was only a cold—he told me his nose had bled at his master's—he went to bed—he awoke me in the night, and his nose was bleeding violently; it poured out all over the bed, I suppose it was more than ten minutes before I could stop it, he went to bed again, it bled again about ten o'clock on the Sunday, and again twice that day, equally violent—he complained of pain in his head—he went to his employment on the Monday, and every day up to the Thursday, but he complained, and was getting worse every day—on the Thursday he came home earlier than usual, and said his head was so bad that he could not do anything at his masters—he went again in the morning, but came home directly, and went to bed—at went again on the Saturday, but he was still very bad, and his master let him come home very early in the evening—he was then very ill indeed, still complaining of his head—he never went to work after that—his master asked him to come on the Monday, and he went, but could not stop—on the Thursday following we called in Mr. Thomas, and he attended him till he died, which
was on Friday, 6th Oct.—I was speaking to him a short time before he died—he told me he was sure he should die that night, and he did die that night—I said to him, "My dear, you are on your death-bed now; are you sure that what you said to those gentlemen, (meaning Mr. Norton, the Magistrate, and his clerk,) was the truth?"—he said, "Yes, aunt; was I to die this moment, what I said was the truth"—several times in the course of his illness he said if he died that boy would be the cause of his death who he had pointed out to me as we were going to Camberwell—the prisoner had passed us, and he said, "That is the boy that hit me on the head."—he did not say that loud enough for the prisoner to hear—he took an oath, when the Magistrate was there, that he never knew a boy of the name of Cole, or Coles—he repeatedly told me during his illness that he never knew nothing of a boy named Cole—he never, at the time he was dying, said anything about a boy named Cole—about half an hour before he died, I asked him whether he had been fighting, and he said, "No, I never have; I never fought with any boy"—I mentioned the name of Cole to him, and he said he did not know a boy of that name.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When was it he pointed out the prisoner? A. On the second Wednesday after he was taken ill—he said, "Aunt, that was the boy that struck me on the head"—I did not know Solita till he pointed him out to me—I did not then know who he was—I did not follow him to find out—the deceased said he lived close by his master's—I did not go to his father, or to the boy, and speak to him—when Mr. Thomas found out what was the matter with him, I went to the police-station—the deceased's mother was ill in bed at the time—Solita's father is a tailor, or draper—I suppose he lived with his father—he had nothing to do with our boy—I swear that he had not been an ailing boy long before the 9th Sept.—he did not wear a handkerchief round his head till after he received the blow; never before that—I have brought him up—I hear that a man has come to swear that he used to wear a handkerchief, and I have a witness to prove he did not—he never cried and complained of his head before—he has said he has had the ear-ache, but never of any consequence—he never cried about it—he has complained but very seldom, when he has had a cold, as many children will—I have noticed a discharge of thick matter from his ear sometimes, but very trifling—I have not done anything for it, but left it to cure itself.
MR. PLATT. Q. Are you a mother? A. No, but I have always been with children—it is not uncommon for children, when they have a cold, to have a discharge at the ear—a healthy child will often have it.
JOHN PETER PERRY . I am clerk, at Lambeth Police-court. The prisoner was taken to the house of the deceased boy's mother, as the boy was too ill to be brought to the office, and this deposition was taken there before Mr. Norton, the Magistrate, who attended—I was present, and the prisoner also—he heard what the deceased stated—it was taken down by me, in presence of the Magistrate, and signed by him—this is his signature—it was read over to the deceased, but he was too ill to sign it—he appeared to me to possess his faculties, and to understand what passed, until the latter part of the deposition; he then became exhausted, and murmured, and seemed to be very anxious that there should be an end of it—at the latter part he mistook Mr. Norton for the surgeon, and asked his permission to have something to drink—when the deposition was concluded he said, "That will do, that will do," rather impatiently; but he was sensible up to that time—the deposition was given, in answer to questions put by Mr. Norton—I correctly took down the
substance of what he said—sometimes the question had to be repeated once or twice—he was exhausted, and had to be raised—it was taken on the 4th Oct.—( deposition read —"Robert Woodrow, on his oath, says: 'I am getting on for thirteen. I swear that that boy hit me; it was on Saturday He hit me with his fist in the back of my head, near the ear. I do not know why he hit me. He hit me with his fist, nothing else; I did not see anything else. I met him opposite Mr. Mayhew's. He did not say anything before he hit me; he hit me hard. He ran after me. I do not know the cause of his hitting me; I had had no quarrel with him. He said nothing to me before he struck me. I knew him before this. I never quarreiled with him. I know his name; it is Adolphus. I have known him a good while, Cross-examined. I had not been fighting the day before with a boy named Cole, in Walworth; I do not know a boy of that name. I had not beer, fighting with anybody. Re-examined. I told my mother of it when I came home.'")—There was a statement made by the prisoner at the police-court—I took it down in the Magistrate's presence, and he signed it—( reads —"I have nothing to say; but one morning he asked me if I would fetch some white mice for him, and I refused. I was coming across the green the last time I saw him before I went into his room. He was at some distance, and did not see me. I did not strike him; I had nothing to strike him for.")
SAMUEL JOHN THOMAS . I am a surgeon, in the London-road. I was seat for, and first visited the deceased on 21st Sept.—he complained of great pain in his head and behind his right ear—I examined it, but perceived nothing, and considered it was a disorder within the head that caused him to complain of pain there—there was no discharge—I went again on the Sunday following, and he then complained of having received a blow—the aunt told me first, and the boy himself afterwards—in consequence of that I examined the parts behind the ear, and on shaving the hair, found a small tumour on the projecting part of the bone—feeling there was matter or some fluid there, I made an incision, and there flowed a small quantity of pus, or matter, very foetid—I saw him daily after that—on the Tuesday following, not considering the incision free enough, I made a larger one, and a small quantity more matter escaped, still very foetid—he gradually got worse—I attended him till he died, which was about twelve, or a little after twelve, on the night of 6th Oct., so 1 was informed—I afterwards made a post mortem examination—on dividing the scalp, I found that portion situated over where the abscess was, in a gangrenous condition, and the pericranium was separated from the skull—it did not adhere as it ought to do—that was precisely over the tumour—the skull beneath the bone, beneath the section of the tumour, was in a state of necrosis or mortification—on examining the inner portion of the skull more particularly, which contains the passage to the internal ear, it was in a very much more advanced state of mortification—the dura mater had a large patch of ulceration upon it immediately beneath the affected portion of the bone and scalp, and on removing the dura mater I found a portion of the bone in a slightly inflamed state—there was a small quantity of reddish white matter on the dura mater, not like that which had escaped from the tumour—that was evidence of decomposition, arising from extended inflammation or fever—in my opinion death was caused by irritative fever, the result of this inflammation and destruction of the parts—there was a small quantity of fluid in the ventricles of the brain, rather more than natural—hearing that he had received a blow, I should ho inclined to suspect that that was the cause of the disease under which he laboured, the fever, the inflammation, and the destruction of
the parts—it might have occurred from spontaneous necrosis, it does so sometimes, without any external injury, if it is in the system—I cannot say that this was caused by external violence, I think it probable, but I could not say so positively—a very slight blow will sometimes produce necrosis in an unhealthy subject— necrosis does not affect the head so frequently as it does other parts of the body, as the legs or arms—the effect of a blow behind the ear would be very sensibly felt, because a variety of important nerves and veins pass in that direction—the discharge from the ear could not of itself cause death, but it might have been the result of a disease which would—he died of mortification—I did not take blood from him, he was not in a condition to bear it, the disease had gone so far—there was no symptom of any discharge in the other ear—I discovered nothing in his system to show a predisposition to any complaint in the ear, but I was not acquainted with his previous history—if he had disease, a blow would probably render it worse and accelerate death.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the boy a strumous or scorbutic subject? A. Not that I am aware of—I cannot tell either one way or the other—if there is a purulent discharge from the ear, that must arise from some disease either within the ear, or deeper—irritative fever is sometimes accompanied by delirium—I only perceived that he was light—headed on one occasion, five or six days before his death, but I heard that he had been delirious in the night—I gave a very small quantity of soothing medicine, not so much to subdue the irritation as from the great disturbance of the bowels—he was suffering from profuse diarrhoea—that would not, if it did not act as intended, produce wandering, not in the small doses I gave it, and combined with other medicines—it will do so sometimes, but the medicine did act as I intended—it was only administered on one or two occasions—I cannot say that the mark behind the ear was produced by a blow—the gangrene covered a space of four inches in length and two in breadth—all the symptoms were those of inflammation.
MR. PLATT. Q. Might not discharge from the ear take place from a common cold? A. Yes, it is frequently the case with children.
JOHN SWAN FLOWERS . I am a surgeon at Camberwell—I assisted Mr. Thomas in the post mortem examination—I have heard his evidence, and concur with him so far as this, that I consider the boy died from irritative or hectic fever, caused by an abscess which had formed on the brain—apart from what we have heard as to a blow, it might have arisen from natural causes, from an abscess generated in the system, without external violence, particularly in a strumous habit of body, or one of delicate formation—if there was a predisposition, a blow would accelerate it—in my opinion disease was existing prior, probably, to the receipt of any blow, and that was indicated by the discharge from the ear—if he had received a blow, and this had been a consequence, I should have anticipated more disturbance of the head, bis intellect would not have been so good as it was, and there would have been vomiting—he ought to have been kept perfectly quiet—if the abscess was caused by a blow, it must have been a very violent one, and in a very bad state of constitution—I should not think a blow from the hand of a boy like the prisoner would produce it.
FRANCES SMITH re-examined. The deceased first mentioned having received the blow the same night, when he came home, and said a boy had struck him—he did not then say what boy, I did not ask him—it was on the Wednesday week afterwards that he pointed out the prisoner—he was very
poorly from the Saturday, till he died—he sunk daily, and was always complaining of his head, and when he was washed he would say, "Don't hurt me where the boy struck me"—still we did not think it was anything further than a cold—I felt the lump there, but I did not believe it was owing to a blow till he pointed out the prisoner and said, "That is the boy that hit my poor head."
STEPHEN HARRIS ( policeman, P 49). The prisoner was given into my charge on 26th Sept.—I told him he was charged with striking a lad named Robert Wood row—he said, "I did not strike him; I never have struck him"—he said that twice, once to me and once to his father.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe his father asked him two or three times, and pressed him very much to tell the truth? A. He did, and he persisted in saying that he did not strike him—he said it very earnestly, and seemed quite surprised when I told him the charge.
SAMUEL JOHN THOMAS re-examined. If this had been produced by a blow, I should have expected the symptoms to have followed rather more quickly—he did alternately get a little better and worse, and there seemed at one time some little probability that he might recover, until diarrhoea set in—he then became exceedingly thirsty, and cried much for ginger-beer, and his friends supplied him rather profusely with it, to pacify him, and that tended to keep up and increase that intestinal irritation, and thereby his condition of course became worse—he sank more rapidly.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18481023-2445]
2445. ELIZA DAVIS, alias Jane King , uttering counterfeit coin, after a previous conviction.
MESSRS. ROBINSON and METCALFE conducted the Prosecution.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . I am assistant-solicitor to the Mint. I produce a copy of the record of Eliza Davis's conviction at this Court, in March, 1847—I have examined it with the original, it is a true copy ( read).
JOHN LEE ( policeman, N 179). I was present when the prisoner was tried in March, 1847—she is the person.
JOHANNA PRIDLEY . I am a widow, and am a cheesemonger at Dock-bead. On Saturday, 16th Sept., between eight and nine in the evening, the prisoner came to my house for a quarter of a pound of butter, and gave me a five-shilling piece—I took twopence-halfpenny, and gave her the change—I did not look at the crown, but threw it into the till—there was another crown there—I took no notice what sort of a crown it was—I afterwards took out two crowns; one was a new Victoria one, and my daughter said, "I took that"—the other was an old one—there were none but those two in the till—I can swear the prisoner is the person—I took particular notice of her.
MARY ANN PRIDLEY . I am daughter of the last witness. On 16th Sept. I received a crown-piece in payment of a bill—it was a Victoria crown—I generally observe the reign of money when I take it—it looked new and bright—I put it into the till—not many minutes after, I saw the prisoner in the shop—I believe my mother was serving her—West the policeman came in soon after she left—this crown is of the same reign as the one I received——I made no mark on it—my attention was called to it by West, not more than five minutes after I put it into the till.
JOHN WEST ( policeman, M 249). On Saturday night, 16th Sept., I saw the prisoner with a man—as they passed Mrs. Pridley's shop I saw them notice it—they passed it about a hundred yards, stopped, talked, and both turned back again; the female on the same side as the shop, and the man on
the opposite side—I saw the prisoner enter Mrs. Pridley's shop, and served with some butter, and take some change—as soon as she left the shop I went in, spoke to Mrs. Pridley, and she gave me these two crown-pieces—I lost sight of the prisoner when I got out of the shop again—I am certain she is the person—I suspected, and watched her.
ELIZABETH PEATMAN . My husband keeps a grocer's-shop at Dock—head. On 16th Sept., about half-past eight, the prisoner came and bought some herrings and onions, which came to twopence—she gave me this crown-piece ( produced), and I gave her the change—I kept the crown in my hand, showed it to my husband, marked it, and gave it to Whitlamb—our shop is not more than two minutes' walk from Mrs. Pridley's.
ISABEL LOUISA COCKLEY . I am the daughter of Mr. Cockley, a cheesemonger, at North-buildings, Bermondsey. On 16th Sept., about a quarter to nine o'clock, the prisoner came, asked for some butter, and tendered me a five-shiiling-piece—I passed it to my mother—she detained it, marked it in my presence, sent for a policeman, and gave the prisoner in custody with the crown.
MARY ANN COCKLEY . On 16th Sept. I received this crown-piece ( produed) from my daughter, and gave it to the policeman—the prisoner was in the shop at the time—I marked it at the station, and gave it to the policeman.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL re-examined. The three crowns are counterfeit, and cast in the same mould—a great deal of the sharpness in the lettering is lost, and at the edges you can see where they have been cut off from the get here is a mark which was on the good piece that made the mould, and it has been transferred to all these three—if they were good that would not occur—many people would take them for good, they do pass into circulation to a very great extent—they are silvered by-means of a voltaic battery.
Prisoner. I beg for mercy; I have a child wholly dependent on me for support; want and bad company are the cause of what I have done.
GUILTY.— Judgment respited.
[trialAccount t18481023-2446]
2446. WILLIAM HUGHES , feloniously cutting and wounding Eliza Moynehan on the head, with intent to do her some grievous bodily harm.
ELIZA MOYNEHAN being called on her recognizance did not appear.
ELEANOR JENKINS . I live at No. 22, Union-square, Tooley-street. Eliza Moynyon lodged with me fifteen months; the prisoner has been in the habit of visiting her for a year—on Tuesday morning, 10th Oct., they both went out about six o'clock, and returned about one, both intoxicated—they went into the room—I heard some quarrelling, I jumped out of bed, and went to try and quiet them—I parted them as well as I could—the woman was undressed and the prisoner was near her—I saw him give her a blow on her head with his fist—she fell on the bed and he kicked her in the head while she was lying there—she was bleeding from the head—I sent for a policeman—two came, and the prisoner was taken in custody, and Moynehan went to the hospital—I cannot be positive how many times he kicked her—he kept kicking her on the head.
Prisoner. I have no recollection of it; I was very much intoxicated. Witness. They lived together peaceably before this—they did have a few-words, but there was no act of violence like this before.
WILLIAM HINCHCLIFF ( policeman, M 85). Between one and two o'clock, on the morning of 10th Oct., I went to Jenkins's house—in consequence of what she told me I went into the back-room, and saw the prisoner holding
himself by the left hand, by the washhand-stand, and kicking Moynehan with the heel of his boot, as she was lying on the bed undressed, with her head towards the bottom—I saw him kick her violently with the heel of his boot twice—she was covered with blood—I seized him, and pulled him away into the next room—I told another constable to take charge of him, for I did not know whether he had killed the woman or not—I then went back, lifted her up, and in about a quarter of an hour she came to herself—I counted seven wounds on her head—I took her to the hospital, and after a week she was let out, to go before the Magistrate, but she never came, and the Magistrate granted a warrant to apprehend her—I found her in Tooley-street, not in the same house—I took her on the warrant, and she gave evidence before the Magistrate, and was bound over to prosecute—I have not seen her since.
GEORGE HOOPER ( policeman M 179). I went to Jenkins's—the prisoner ran out of the door—I pursued him bout 100 yards, and took him into custody—he ran very fast indeed—he did not appear drunk—he asked me what I took him for.
ALGERNON SUDLOW . I am house-surgeon at Guy's Hospital. Eliza Moynehan was brought there—I examined her head, and found seven incised wounds, which seemed to have been bleeding profusely—blood was still oozing from some of them, and there was a great deal of contusion round some of them—she appeared very much excited—I have seen the prisoner's boots—they are mounted with iron, and might make the wounds—a person leaning on a washhand-stand and kicking behind him, would be capable of kicking with considerable force—the woman was in the hospital seven or eight days, and attended twice afterwards as an out-patient—I did not examine the depth of all the wounds; I do not think the bone was reached—she went on very well indeed—she was in a very healthy state of body—erysipelas might have followed—when she first came she was quite insensible, and had the appearance of being drunk—that might be caused by the blows she had received—she complained of headache.
Prisoner's Defence. We have been together very comfortable since, and always were before, except for drink.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 27.— Confined Nine Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

2448. THOMAS PARSONS , stealing 19lbs. weight of pot-metal, and 1 metal waste, value 8 s.; the goods of William Pontifex and another, his masters.
JOHN SHARP . I am manager to William and Edward Pontifex, copper-smiths and engineers. The prisoner has been in their employ about fourteen years—on the evening of 13th Oct. I saw him leaving the premises, and I kept him in conversation to point him out to the officer—I have seen the metal produced—it is called pot-metal—I have no doubt it is the property of Messrs. Pontifex—I can swear to this article, it is a rough casting—it is never sold or sent out in the state it is now in.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What is it called? A. Waste—it is part of a washer and waste—it is made to fix in a washer—I cannot say that I had seen this on the premises.
JAMES BURTON ( policeman M 272). I watched the prisoner—I saw him come out of the factory—I took him in a passage in Gravel-lane, Southwark, at the back of a marine-store dealer's shop—I told him I suspected he had got some property he had brought from his employers'—he said, "It is a bad job, it is the first lot I brought from my employers"—I found this metal on him, some in his hat, and some in different pockets.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 52.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Four Months.

2450. WILLIAM HISCOCK , stealing 2 shillings, 3 sixpences, 2 pence, and 3 halfpence; the moneys of George Wells, his master.
GEORGE WELLS . I keep the Coopers' Arms, at Putney. The prisoner was in my service—on 26th Sept. I marked some money, and put it in different places where I had lost money from before—I put 16 s. 6 d. into a bag in a drawer, four sixpences in the till, and 1 s. worth of halfpence—I locked up the bar and the places—the next morning at eight o'clock. I came, and missed two shillings and a sixpence out of one place, and 3 1/2 d. out of another—the money was all found on the prisoner by the officer—we went up stairs, and found in his box brandy and wine and cheroots, and a great many other things.
CHARLES CANHAM ( policeman, V 58). I was secreted in the house—I saw the prisoner come down at half-past seven o'clock in the morning—he went into the bar-parlour—I heard a drawer and a till go—I waited till Mr. Wells came—I told him, and this money was gone—we found the marked money on the prisoner—I had seen it marked—it was the same money—I found this other property in the prisoner's box.
Prisoner's Defence. I opened the door: I saw some money down on the mat, and took it up. (The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined One Year .

2451. MARY DONOVAN , stealing 6 sheets, and 1 gown, value 14 s.; the goods of William Poole and another; having been previously convicted.
WILLIAM POOLE . I am a pawnbroker, and have one partner. On 11th Oct. the prisoner came to our house, and I saw her take down a gown, and conceal it beneath her clothes, and a bundle of sheets, and put them under her shawl—she left the shop, saying she would call again in the afternoon—I followed her, and asked her to walk back with me, and I saw her drop these things from under her shawl, and from under her petticoats—I had not sold them to her—she had no right to them.
Prisoner's Defence. I had had a little too much to drink, and he chaffed roe; I could not have-stolen them; I beg for mercy, on account of my poor children; I never was in the hands of a policeman but once before; I have worked nine years for one master.
JOHN DELANY ( policeman, M 100). I produce a certificate of the prisoner's
former conviction—( read—Convicted Jan., 1848: confined twenty-one days)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Nine Months.
