[trialAccount t18481023-2458]
2458. WILLIAM PARSONS , stealing 1 purse, value 6 d.; 1 pocket, 6 d.; 32 sovereigns, and 15 shillings; the property of Sarah Adams.
SARAH ADAMS . I am single, and lodge at 103, Blackfriars-road. I have been in service in France—I came to London, and met the prisoner on 3rd Oct.—I asked him to show me a lodging—I had then thirty-two sovereigns—he took me to 61, White Horse-street—he told me I was quite safe, as if I was in my own house—he left me about an hour and a half—he came back, and took my pocket off the chair—I hallooed, and told him to put it down—he put his hand into it, and took the money—I got up and seized him by the wrists—he twisted himself from me, got away, and ran out of the door—I begged him to come back, but he would not—I returned, and called the person of whom I took the room—she said she knew him—he was one of her father's clubmates—she took me to a house, but it was the wrong person.
Prisoner. Q. What time did you meet me? A. About twenty minutes to nine o'clock, you asked me to go into a public-house—I do not know what house it was—I was drinking there between eight and nine—I was not asleep when you took the money—there was a light in the room.
COURT. Q. How did you come by the money? A. I had been saving it up five years—I left my master's last July—I was not going to sleep with
the prisoner—I never saw him before—he was sitting half an hour with me, assuring me that I was safe.
MARIA MAYHEW . My father keeps a lodging-house, in White Horse-Street. The prisoner introduced the prosecutrix between eight and nine o'clock, and asked if I had got a bed for the night—I went down and got a light, and took them up to the room—he had been up about half an hour—he then came over to the opposite house to me about ten—he was out about an hour and a half or two hours—he came in again, and was up stairs five minutes, he came down, and asked if I would have something to drink—I said, "No"—he pulled me to go, and take something—I would not—he said it was all right, he was a club-mate of my father's—when I went up with him first I asked him to turn the key of the door in the morning as the landlord did not lodge in the house—he said he should not go till half-past eight—if the prosecutrix had come to the door I should have seen her, because I was opposite—I did not hear of the robbery till the morning, when she came and said, she had been robbed of 32 l. 15 s., and asked if I should know the man—I said, "Yes, he told me he was a club-mate of my father's"—I gave my father the description of him in the morning, and he told me it was another man.
JAMES FROST ( policeman, L 59). I took the prisoner in bed the next night at Broadwall, four or five hundred yards from Mayhew's—while I was. searching, the prosecutrix came and wished to compromise the case.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.

2459. THOMAS JOHNSON , stealing 1 firkin, value 6 d.; 90 lbs. Weight of butter, 3 l.; the goods of Edmund Goodman, having been before convicted; to which he pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18481023-2460]
2460. EDWARD JOHNSON, WILLIAM JONES , and ROBERT HANLON , stealing 1 pocket-book, value 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of James Saxby Jarvis. from his person; Johnson having been before convicted; to which
JONES pleaded GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Nine Months.
JAMES SAXBY JARVIS . I live at Union-place, Lambeth. On 26th Sept., in the evening, I was in Black friars-road—as I turned the corner of Stamford-street, I saw the prisoners larking —a little farther on Johnson came before me—I pushed him on—he kept before me—I shoved him aside once or twice—Hanlon was on my left—Johnson stopped short, got under my legs and tried to lift me up—I felt something touch my pocket and took hold of Jones's hand with my pocket-book in it—Hanlon was holding my pocket up—he ran away—I found him next morning at the station—I secured Jones, two gentlemen stopped Johnson—his head was tied up then, and I know him well.
AMBROSE FURMINGO ( policeman, L 162). I was on duty, and saw Jones and Johnson together about an hour before the robbery—I took them from Mr. Jarvis.
WILLIAM POUND . I am a shoemaker of Duke-street, Westminster. I was a witness against Johnson by the name of Benjamin Lawson, and produce a certificate of his conviction—( read—Convicted September, 1847, and confined three months)—he is the person.
JOHNSON — GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Nine Months.
HANLON — GUILTY , and received a good character.—Aged 16.— Confined Fourteen Days .
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.

[trialAccount t18481023-2462]
2462. JOHN MCCARTHY , feloniously cutting and wounding George Quinnear upon his head, with intent to do him grievous bodily harm.—2nd COUNT, to resist and prevent the lawful apprehension of a person unknown.
MESSRS. BODKIN and CLERK conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM SMITH ( policeman, P 32). I was on duty on 7th Oct., about seven o'clock, in the Walworth-road, with George Quinnear—we were in plain clothes—I saw the prisoner with another man, who had his hand in a lady's pocket—the prisoner was walking close behind him, covering him—I said I am a constable—I took the prisoner, Quinnear took hold of the other—the prisoner got away from me—I lost sight of him for a minute or two—I communicated with the lady—while the other man was scuffling with Quinnear, the prisoner returned, went up, and struck Quinnear on the head with what had the appearance of a small stick—he immediately fell—I caught the prisoner striking a second blow; he struck me with this instrument on the forehead—I saw blood come from Quinnear—several persons were behind, beating me about the head—my hat was broken all to pieces—the stick appeared about a foot and a half long—the blow appeared to be more severe than a stick of that length would give, that was not weighted at the end.
GEORGE QUINNEAR ( policeman, C 201). I was with Smith, and saw the prisoner and the man with him, with his hand in the lady's pocket—I took that man—I was struggling with him—he was trying to shift out of his coat—the prisoner came up—he took a short stick from his dress, and gave me a deliberate and violent blow on the head, which broke my hat and cut my head—I was felled to the ground—the stick appeared to be very heavy at the end—I still kept hold of the man, and as I was falling the prisoner struck me again on the shoulder, and the other man escaped, and left his coat and hat behind—I bled, and was taken to the doctor's—I had told the man I was a police-officer—I have been unfit for duty till this time.
ENOCH DAWSON HOWITT . I am a surgeon. On 7th Oct. Quinnear was brought to my house—I examined his head, and found a clean incised wound, about three-quarters of an inch long, such as might have been inflicted by a severe blow from a stick—it corresponded with the holes in the hat—it would require a heavy blow to cut through the hat, and produce that wound—if the stick had not been loaded, I should say it would not have done it.
Prisoner. Some one struck me on the side of my head, I turned and struck the officer; I did not know he was an officer; I never had a stick; there were two witnesses who have not been called.
WILLIAM HINTON examined by the Prisoner. I saw you strike with you: fist, but not with a stick—you had a stick in the first instance.
THOMAS BARBER . I saw Smith take you, and you struck him two or three times with your fist.
GUILTY . ** Aged 27.— Transported for Ten Years .
[trialAccount t18481023-2463]
2463. EDWIN JOSEPH LUCAS , stealing 3lbs. of printing type, leads, and other articles, value 6 s.; the goods of Henry Silverlock, his master.
MR. PARRY conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY SILVERLOCK . I am a printer in Wardrobe-terrace, Doctors'-commons. The prisoner was in my employ as overseer or foreman—his wages were 50 s. a week—he had access to all parts of my office and stock—I employ sixty or seventy men—I went with the policeman to the prisoner's house in Blackfriars-road, on 26th Sept.—one officer went in the back premises with my foundry-man, and there found some metal lead—I did not go with him—I found this metal block, or moulding, in the front room, on the first-floor—I believe it to be mine—this cast is made use of in the working of the wrapper of the Patent Journal —that is a work that has been printed on my premises—the prisoner would have no business to take any type or mould from my premises to his own—this is stereotyped—I had printed the Journal about a year and a half—I was not present when the prisoner was given into custody—I had not charged him with it before I went to his premises.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long had he been in your service? A. About two years—I printed a work called the Nautical Standard —the prisoner was not originally employed in my jobbing business, but on the Nautical Standard —he was put on it about April—that was what he first came to work upon—he had no other employ to attend to then—he was I think on piece work—he earned about 36 s. a week—Jones, my overseer left me, and the prisoner being a steady young man I put him in the place, and increased his wages to 50 s.—Jones' wages were 60 s. a week—he was only overseer, the prisoner was overseer, and had the printing of the Nautical Standard —Jones also had the superintendence of that—Mr. Barlow was the proprietor of the journal—the prisoner was not in custody when we searched his house—he was in my employ—I took my founder, Hicks, with me to the prisoner's premises—I did not give the prisoner any notice, or make any charge against him at that time—I ceased to be printer of the Patent Journal the week before—I knew when I went to search his premises on the 26th, that I was no longer to be continued the printer of the Patent Journal —I gave the prisoner notice to quit my employ on the 18th Sept.—I did not at that time know that he was going to be taken into the employ of Messrs. Barlow and Co., to be employed on the Patent Journal —I knew it on the Friday following, before I searched his house—all that I can recognise of these articles amount to 6 s. in value—the printing of the Patent Journal did produce a profit, but a very small one—I believe this block to be mine, as far as a man can speak to property on his premises—I mean to swear to it—it had been made for myself—I know a person named Miller—( looking at a sheet)—this is not the impression of my block—nor is this other—( looking at another)—here is part of it here—this one has been cut and pieced about—this centre appears to be it, but the top and bottom have both been cut off—Miller had nothing to do with this casting—he had to do with some former blocks—these were at one time in separate casts—they were sent to me to work, and I cast them for my own convenience—the small blocks I have no doubt went hack to Mr. Barlow, or Mr. Miller as their agent, but this block was never out of my premises—I know that Capsey and Cole, two men who were in my service, lodged in the same house with the prisoner—I did not employ them, or either of them to make any search at the house—after I heard that he was going on the Patent Journal they went there, being anxious to find out the guilt—I told all the men in my employ that property was missing, and every man must look out—I deny that I gave them authority, or direction to make any search there—I believe Capsey and Cole, and the prisoner all left my service together—it was not at my instance that Capsey and Cole left, nor by any interference of mine—I
had no reason to believe before I gave the prisoner into custody that he had received this block from Miller's hand—I had never been told so before he was in custody—in coming out of the Court, Miller said, "That has been in my possession"—I learnt with utter astonishment from Miller, when I was at the station, or police-court, that he had given this to the prisoner—he did not say he gave it to the prisoner—he said it had been in his possession—I knew the prisoner was about to be employed on the Patent Journal, and that Miller told me it had been in his hands—I did not ask whether Miller gave it him—from a memorandum found, it appears that Miller gave it him on the previous day—this plate would be of no use to anybody that did not print the Patent Journal —Messrs. Barlow and others were about to employ the prisoner to print it.
MR. PARRY. Q. Look at this other property, which do you identify? A. This is a plate that is partly melted—it is a stereotype plate of "genuine Court-plaister"—these leads have a particular mark on them—here is a little round mark, slightly indented, which arises from a little defect in the mould—almost every one of these has the mark—this one I cannot identify, but ray foundryman does.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. I believe you stated it to be yours from the remaining impression of the plug mark? A. Yes, and no other—on this piece of lead which has been partly melted", there is the stamp of Court-plaister—if he had taken any impression of one of my leads for the purpose of enabling him to make more by it, it would not have the same impression that mine has—it is too fine a mould—I did not employ a reporter to take down the evidence before the Magistrate, and afterwards publish it—I had a communication with a gentleman who sits below me—the second day we were at the office he came there—I had seen and known him some years—my communication to him was that I had been robbed, and that it was coming on at the police-court, and he was to come down—he was not to take a report—there was a reporter there—I did not employ him to report the case—there was another reporter at the office, but 1 was not acquainted with matters of this kind—a reporter reporting at a police-court must know these things—I never was in a police court—I knew there was a regular reporter there, and I told this gentleman, and he came—I will not say that I did, or did not tell him to come—I do not believe that I did—I have no recollection that I did—I had continual communication with him in the police-court while the case was going on—that person did not report the case—the case was reported by another reporter—I think it was not reported by him on the second occasion—I do not think he would have made the mistakes that were made—I did not speak to him till the case had been before the Magistrate.
Q. Just understand me; supposing one of these to be used as a model, and put into a mould, would not the impression it made have this effect, that if metal were poured into the mould, it would come out with the same deficiencies as this—it would make an entire assimilation of it? A. No, because casting in a metal mould would be entirely different from a sand mould—I know the prisoner was casting, because it was found on bis premises—the Queen's Arms on the "Court-plaister" stamp had never been thrown into the refuse box to my knowledge—I do not know that any of these had been worn enough to have been put into the waste metal box—they might have been condemned by the printer, and then they would be, but it so happened that this was not printed lately—these lead types I charge to be of the value of 1 s. 6 d., the mould casting 2 s., the Court-plaister and the King's Arms 2 s., and the brass rules at 6 d.; making altogether 6 s.—I do not know that the
prisoner has been in the habit of taking metals for the purpose of casting—I never heard from any of my servants that he was in the habit of taking these sort of things one at a time for the purpose of moulding and returning them—I had been told so by one, that is a witness named Fall, a few days back—I cannot say when—he took me, and pointed out some of the very things that he had taken from my premises to mould.
MR. PARRY. Q. Before this charge was preferred, had you missed property? A. I had—I have no idea of the amount, there are many tons of metal, it would be impossible—the whole value I charge is 6 s.—the value is depreciated by being fused—this metal might be fused metal-type—I do not identify this as my property—a man has shown me some casting of the prisoner's—this is a portion of a stereotype-plate—part of it has been melted down—that is an act which the prisoner would have no authority to do—the part that has been separated on this block, is the writing part at the top and bottom—this reporter is a friend of mine—I did not see him' take a note—there is not the slightest pretence for saying that I employed a reporter—the prisoner was going to be employed on the Patent Journal —there is not the slightest pretence for saying that it was in consequence of that I took him.
EDWARD HICKS . I reside in Vine-street, Waterloo-road, and am a moulder, in the service of Mr. Silverlock—I have been with him ten years next May—I have examined these plates and block—this block is my master's property—this is a plate of my casting, I am not positive when I cast it, but I think in Sept., 1847, or about that time—this plate, which has been partly melted, that has "Court-plaister" on it, answers to one that I have missed a long time, and here is the back of the other—there were two missing, and these answer to them—here are some leads that I know—they have the mark of a plug which was fixed in a damaged place in the mould—here is a particular mark on this Patent Journal stereotype—I was" sent for by Mr. Lucas—he complained that it printed very thick, and he asked me to clear it out, which I did, and there is a nail-mark which has appeared in the printing for five or six months—here are the Royal arms—this is a cast of mine, taken from a large bill—here is the bill of it—I know these brass rules by the nicks at the back—they are not all nicked in this way: some are—I know them, they were cut for the Literary Gazette —they are all marked, but not all in the centre—as it regards these leads, I have the mould here in which they were cast—I can identify them by the plug-mark in this mould—these are the leads that were cast in this mould, and by me—here is the plug-mark, and there is the same mark in the mould—there might be a mould taken of one of these leads, and then it would be recast, but then it would be a lighter colour—if this were recast I do not believe it would have the plug-mark on it in the same way it has now, it is so slight.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose there were thousands of pounds of property of your master's about? A. Yes—I cast these leads in this mould, and they were cut by a knife, not by a matrix—I do not know that it was known that the prisoner was going to be employed on the Patent Journal —I was not aware of anything of the kind till after he was in custody—I could not say who it was I learned it from—I do not know that it was known in the establishment that the prisoner was in the habit of taking these sort of things to take copies from—I did not know that he had found out a means of making these leads in a sand mould—I have not been told so, but I have been given to understand it, from what I saw on his premises—I was sent with the officer on 26th Sept.—I cannot say that I routed his premises all over—I brought all that you see here—I did not find, when I searched his premises,
the identical mould from which the leads were made—I had the mould myself—I saw a moulding-frame, but not a mould for making patterns of these leads—I saw moulding-frames—if ever they had been used with sand, they were without sand at that time—I went back to his house on the same day, the 26th—I did not go afterwards—I know Cole and Capsey, from their being employed on the premises—this is not the moulding-frame I saw; it was a larger one than this—I saw two moulding-frames—I cannot say whether the Court-plaister stamp, or the Queen's Arms' stamp have ever been in the waste-metal box—the Court-plaister wrapper has been used many times—when articles are put in the waste-metal box it is for the purpose of being melted again by me—I claim all these brass rules that are nicked, as being nicked by me—the men do not carry these sort of rules in their pockets—what they call the stick-rule they do—it is not common for them to carry these about them, and carry them home, and return them—these were prepared fur printing the Literary Gazette, which had ceased to be printed—I was authorized by my employer, a few days after several ornaments were suspected to be taken out of the box, to look over the ornaments, and I did so two days afterwards—I never had any authority to see if I could make any case against the prisoner by questioning the men, before any charge was made we had conversation—I did not question any of the men at all—I did not know that the prisoner was in the habit of casting from plates of Mr. Silverlock's.
WILLIAM GUEST ( policeman, L 62). On 26th Sept. I went with another officer with a search-warrant to the prisoner's house in Blackfriars'-road—I found these Royal arms in the cupboard, and these four or five pieces of brass.
HENRY ROWE ( City policeman, 356). I found these leads in the outhouse—there was a fire-place there—I produce a diary—the prisoner pulled it on. of his pocket, and tore these leaves out, and tried to swallow them; he got them to his mouth—I seized his throat and prevented him.
Cross-examined. Q. You were examined before the Magistrate? A. Yes—I did not say one word about this—I was not asked.
HENRY CAPSEY . I am a compositor. I have been about a year and a half in the employ of Mr. Silverlock—I can speak to these leads and this brass-rule—I reside in the prisoner's house, in Blackfriars-road; there is an outhouse in the yard—I remember my master complaining of things being lost about the 12th or 14th Sept.—on Thursday night, the 14th Sept., about nine o'clock, or from that to ten, I saw through the window of the room where I reside a light proceeding from the upper story in the prisoner's outhouse—I went down into the yard, and while 1 was there the prisoner came down out of the place—he said, "Halloo, Capsey, are you there?"—I said, "Yes"—he said, "I have got a bit of a job up stairs at tinkering; I am mending the leg of a chair"—I said, "You are fond of tinkering"—I had observed a vast volume of smoke proceeding from the chimney, and I knew there was a fire—I smelt fumes, like molten metal; I had smelt them before—on the night of the 15th we had been at work at the Nautical Standard, and on Saturday morning, the 16th, as we did not go to work so early as usual, I observed the prisoner come out of the outhouse, and there was a smoke, and 1 observed the smell of the metal—the prisoner said he had got a fire up stairs, and he had been burning an old mattress that had bugs in it—on the morning of the 26th Sept. I got up early, on account of my wife being unwell, I went down into the yard, the outhouse was unfastened, and I went into it; I saw a quantity of leads on the floor, I saw a fire-place, and a pan, or sort of thing in it, for melting metal, I took up three of the leads, and examined them, I brought
them to my master—it was in consequence of things being missed that I noticed the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. Who did miss them? A. A man named Robinson—I was a lodger of the prisoner's, and so was Cole—I did not give notice to quit the premises prior to going—I had no conversation with Mr. Silverlock about them—I left without notice, and so did Cole—I have never paid the rent at all; I have never been applied to for it.
Witnesses for the Defence.
THOMAS EARL MILLER . I am collector for Messrs. Barlow, the proprietors of the Patent Journal. I know this stereotype-plate; it was used for printing the Patent Journal —these small parts were sent to Mr. Silverlock—I know this block: I handed it to the prisoner on the Monday evening before he was in custody—this would be used for printing that journal.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. When did you give it him? A. On Monday evening, the 23rd or 24th Sept.—it was the day before he was in custody—I took it off the shelf in our office—it was sent to our office with several other blocks—Mr. Silverlock had printed the Journal for two years—this is only used occasionally, and when it is not used, it is sent to oar office to be put aside till it is wanted again—it was to be wanted for the following Saturday, and for that reason I gave it him—I did not know that he had an office—I gave it to him personally in our office in Chancery-lane—it was my orders from Mr. Barlow that Lucas was to print it that week, and I was to give it to him.
---- BEAUMONT. I am in the service of Messrs. Wright and Son, metal-merchants. On the 19th July I sold some metal, I believe, to the prisoner—I sold a crucible also—this is the bill of it ( looking at it).
( MR. PARRY. here withdrew from the Prosecution.)
NOT GUILTY .

2464. EDWIN GATEHOUSE and WILLIAM DOWLEY , unlawfully meeting together for the purpose of committing b----y.
DOWLEY — GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined One Year .
GATEHOUSE — GUILTY . Aged 30.
[trialAccount t18481023-2465]
2465. EDWIN GATEHOUSE was again indicted for stealing 2 pairs of shoes, value 105.; and other goods, 1 l. 15 s.; the goods of James Towers, His master.
MR. SLEIGH conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM ROMAINE ( police-sergeant, L 38). I went with Vine, during the last month, to a shoe-shop, 133, Lambeth-walk, kept by the prisoner—I made a search over the stock—Vine afterwards selected from that stock property which he stated was his roaster's—I have it here.
Cross-examined by MR. CARTER. Q. When you went to the house was the prisoner there? A. No, he was in custody—these shoes were in the shop, publicly exposed for sale—these boot-trees were found at the further end of the shop—the prisoner's wife and her mother were in charge of the shop—the prisoner did not live with his wife—he used to lock the place up at night, and go to sleep at the other house—his wife was locked in when I went.
JAMES TOWERS . I am a boot and shoemaker, and live in Tottenham Court-road. I have two shops in Whitecross-street—in Aug., 1846, I employed the prisoner in one shop—it was his duty to attend to it, and the (roods—he had the charge of the whole shop, and the property in it—I had in my other shop a shopman, named Vine—I discharged the prisoner on the 17th July—I cannot identify any of this property; a great many goods of this description are made by a person in Northamptonshire—these are the
same sort of goods that I sent the prisoner to sell in my shop—I have lost property—I was 65 l. 5 s. 2 d. deficient in my stock.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the prisoner the only person who had change of the property in the shop? A. Yes, he had the whole power of disposing of the property; he had to dispose of it in the best manner he could for my interest—I understand he had opened another shop three months before, but I did not hear of it till three days before the 17th July—I took a person with me to take stock on the 17th July—I found the deficiency was 69 l. 5 s. 2 d.—I made the charge against the prisoner on that day—I used to give him an account of what the stock ought to be, and he said it agreed—I have supplied him with stock every week, and he said it was correct—I supplied him with about 90 l. worth of stock at the commencement—if I had the books here I could tell you—I only attended on those premises now and then, once in two or three weeks—he gave me an account every week of what goods he sold, and handed me over the money—on taking stock 1 told him he was so much short—I did not give him in charge.
HENRY VINE . I was shopman to Mr. Towers—tbe prisoner was also a shopman—he conducted the business of one shop, and I another—I went to the prisoner's shop, in Lambeth-walk, with the policeman—I found some property there, which I believe to be my master's—these two pairs of shoes I believe to be my master's, by their particular make and the private mark—this is not my mark—here is one pair that have my own private mark on them—I have seen them in my master's possession—this tree is my master's; we have the remaining portion of it, and the same with respect to this other piece of the tree, I found the whole of them in the prisoner's house.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you not yourself been charged with stealing some of the goods? A. No; nothing of the kind—these two shops were in the same street—we were in the habit of going to each other's shops, and obtaining what we required—one pair of these were in my possession at Whitecross-street—I identify them through their having been in my hands, and then going into the prisoner's hands—I found them on the prisoner's premises—I will swear I have not sold these shoes—they were manufactured in Northampton, but they have my mark on them, in my handwriting—the prisoner had these in exchange from me, for his master, they still remaining Mr. Tower's property—this is the mark, I am prepared to swear to it—these others I believe to be my master's—my belief is founded on the style of the shoes—they have no mark, the mark is scratched out—I went with the officer to the house in Lambeth-walk—there were no articles there for sale then—I found some of these in Piccadilly, and some at Lambeth-walk.
WILLIAM ROMAINE re-examined. Some of these were found in a court in Piccadilly—they were taken from Lambeth-walk there, in a van—I was present when they were removed—I could not swear that this is one pair that was removed, but there were a great many removed, and Vine was taken to Piccadilly to look at these.
DAVID LANGSTON . I am a butcher, and live at 135, Lambeth-walk. I let part of a shop to the prisoner, which he kept for selling shoes—there were a parcel of shoes in his shop, which were packed up, and taken away in a little van.
Cross-examined. Q. Who took the premises? A. The prisoner—he slept there four or five nights—his wife and his mother slept there, and a child—he has been in the occupation since May—I was present when these shoes were removed—I believe his wife directed them to be taken—he was then in custody on a charge of misdemeanor.
COURT. Q. Did he come every day to transact the business of the shop? A. Yes; he took the prominent part in the business—the boot-trees were selected from the further end of the shop, and the shoes from the stock in Piccadilly.
(Henry Chesland, a smith, and William Wesley, an agent for the Northampton boot and shoe manufactory, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Transported for Seven Years .
[trialAccount t18481023-2466]
2466. JOHN DONOVAN , for a robbery, (with three persons unknown,) on John Chambers; putting him in fear, and stealing from his person, and against his will, I watch, value 2 l.; and striking, beating, and using other personal violence to him; having been before convicted.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN CHAMBERS . I live in the Old Kent-road—I am traveller to Mr. Joseph Lloyd, of Duke-street. On the evening of 11th Oct. I was walking in Willow-walk, Bermondsey, at a little after eight o'clock, near the Greyhound-yard—I was seized from behind, as it appeared, by more than one person—they put their hands over my eyes—after knocking me down, they let me go, and went away—while they were about it, one of them said, "Quick! quick!"—I was then knocked down, and they ran off—there were four of them—they turned to the left, towards the Grange-road—I got up and called "Thieves!" and "Police!"—I had lost my watch during the time they had been pressing on my throat—I saw the policeman get over the back premises of Alfred-terrace—I saw Mr. Maeer—he called out, and I went round to the front of Mr. Maeer's house, and saw the prisoner in custody of the police—he asked why he was taken, and said he went over there for a necessary purpose—my watch was found in the garden adjoining the house—this is it ( produced).
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How far is the place where you lost your watch from the place where you saw the policeman getting over the garden-fence? A. Perhaps 700 or 800 yards—you go to the bottom of Grange-road, and turn to the right, and then to the left—I did not see the policeman till I saw him getting over that fence.
JOHN SURMAN . I live in Caroline-street, Old Kent-road, with my parents. I am employed daily by Mr. Mullins, a surgeon. On Wednesday night, 11th Oct., between eight and nine o'clock, I heard a cry of "Police!" coming from Willow-walk—it was moonlight—I stopped and looked down the walk—I saw a gentleman lying down, and four men upon him, stooping and bending over him—I saw one of them feeling about his clothes—I noticed that that one had no whiskers—it was the prisoner—I am sure he is the man—I saw the men who were there going away from the gentleman when they heard me cry "Police!"—I ran up toward? the place, and saw the four men turn round the Grange-road—the prisoner was one of them—they ran down Queen's-road, down Alfred-street, and got over toe back wall in Alfred-terrace—there is a high heap of dirt there, I got on it, and saw three of the men go to the right, over the Palings, and I saw the prisoner go into Mr. Maeer's garden down by toe kitchen door—I saw a policeman getting over another garden, and I cried out, "Here he is; here he is," pointing to Mr. Maeer's garden—I saw Mr., Maeer come out of his door, and presently the door was opened and a light was brought, and I saw the policeman go down to the kitchen door with the prisoner—I followed him to the station, and recognised him as being the person—I have no doubt of his being the person.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you notice the other men? A. I did when they turned the corner, I saw two of the others had whiskers, I do not know what the other had—when this was done, I was passing the end of Willow-walk—I cannot tell how far I was off exactly; twenty-four yards, I daresay—when I was calling, "Police!" I ran towards the bridge, and the four men ran towards the toll-gate, and when I saw them running I followed—if this man had not been taken that night I should have been able to recollect him—I should know him by his clothes—he had a green waistcoat on—I was not acquainted with him before—I saw him taken in the garden—I could see that, because I was on a high heap of dirt at the back of Alfred-terrace—I did not know the policeman before this time—I am twelve years old—I never was a witness before—I work regularly for Mr. Mullins.
JOSEPH MAEER . I took the prisoner that night in my wash-house—he could not have got there without getting over a paling six feet high—there were two fences for him to get over—he said he came there for a necessary purpose.
Cross-examined. Q. Could he not get there without going over a fence six feet high? A. No; not without opening the front-door of the houses—I cannot say whether there is any dirt outside.
JANE BEUZE . My little boy found this watch in my presence on the left hand side of the garden—I know where the prisoner was found; it was about twenty yards from there—he would not have to pass that place—he must have thrown it over.
JOHN WRIGHT ( policeman, M 63). I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted 6 th July, 1846, transported for seven years)—the prisoner is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Fifteen Years .
[trialAccount t18481023-2467]
2467. DAVID PRICE and WALTER JAMES SUNTER , for a robbery on William Nunn, putting him in fear, and stealing from his person 1 watch, value 3 l., and 1 chain, value 1 s., his goods.
MR. THOMPSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM NUNN . On Sunday night, 22nd Oct., I was in Tower-street, Westminster-road—Price came up to me and said, "How are you?"—I said, "Very well, how are you?"—he said, "Who would have thought of seeing you here"—I said, "I don't know that any one did"—he said, "Thomas, you certainly know me"—I said, "No; nor you don't know me; nor I don't want to have anything to say to you"—he directly put his hand to my pocket—I put my hand to my trowsers pocket, and five or six came round me, and one of them snatched my watch from my pocket—I heard the guard snap from my neck—when I came to myself a little from the struggle, Sunter had got hold of my stock, and shoved me backwards against the rail, and Price was standing by the side of him.
Cross-examined by MR. SLEIGH. Q. What time was this? A. Half-past eleven at night—I am not married—I had not been talking to any one before this occurrence took place—I was walking by myself to the best of my knowledge—I had not been in company with some women just previously—I swear I was not outside a public-house speaking to some women—I never saw either of the prisoners before that night—there might be a good many men in the street—there were five or six round me—I will not swear there were not a dozen round me—it was not very dark, nor yet light—I had had part of a pot of beer in the Oxford Arms, in the same road in which I was robbed—I did
not go into more than one public-house—I cannot tell what time I went into the public-house—I was not there above twenty minutes.
COURT. Q. Were you sober? A. Yes; I am able to swear that these are the men that robbed and assaulted me—it was not a quarter of a minute after my watch was taken that the prisoners were taken by Mr. Watts.
HENRY HOLLYMAN . On that night I was in Tower-street, Westminster-road about a quarter past twelve o'clock—I was about three yards on this side of the Oxford Anns—I heard a female call out, "Thomas!"—I turned and it was to Sunter she said, " Tom, mind the hole"—I saw Price speak to the prosecutor—he said, " Tom, how are you?"—he said, "I know nothing of you"—I turned down a street, and heard a scuffle—I came back and saw Sunter had got the prosecutor up against the fence, and a man and woman ran by me—Mr. Watts took Sunter, and he said he knew nothing about it.
Price. When I put my hand to the prosecutor's pocket, why did he not give me in charge?
WILLIAM NUNN re-examined. Because I was so confounded—I had not two girls under my arm—I had no girls that night.
HENRY HOLLYMAN re-examined. When I saw the prosecutor in the first instance he had one woman leaning on his arm.
JOEL WATTS . I live in Battersea-fields. On Sunday week I was returning home with my nephew, William Nunn, and a man and a woman—I was twenty yards a head of them—I heard my nephew call out for assistance—I went back and saw the two prisoners had hold of him—I collared them both and held them till a policeman came—I gave them in charge.
Prices Defence. I am innocent; this gentleman said, "Have you seen any one come this way?" I said, "No;" I came across the road; I was just going home; I saw the prosecutor come out with two females; I went and had half a quartern of gin and was going home; I was standing at the corner half a minute and heard a row; I ran down the street and saw the prosecutor bustling about; I made answer to him, "What is the matter, Thomas?" he said, "What is that to you? I will give you in charge"—I said, "It is time enough when I have done anything."
(Henry Crook, a builder;——Parson, a smith and engineer; and Walter Hirris, a bookbinder, gave Sunter a good character).
PRICE — GUILTY .
SUNTER — GUILTY .
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18481023-2468]
2468. WILLIAM PINGO , for a robbery on James Colledge, and stealing from his person 1 watch, value 10 l.; I chain, value 6 d.; and 1 key, value 2 d.; his goods—and SARAH THOMAS , feloniously receiving the same.
MR. SLEIGH conducted the Prosecution:
JAMES COLLEDGE . I live in Amelia-street, Walworth-road, I hare been a beer-seller. On the night of 17th Oct., I went with the prisoners to the Fed Lion, and had some drink—they had been customers of mine—from there we went to the Blue Coat Boy, and had some drink, and from there to a house in East-lane—my wife came and asked me to go home—both prisoners want borne with me—Thomas went out with my wife and left Pingo and me—he struck me—that deprived me of my senses, and when I recovered I found my waistcoat opened and my watch gone—I said,. "Where is my wach?"—he said, "You have hid it"—I said, "No, I have not; you shall not go till it is found"—Thomas came in—I was then in the passage minding the loor—I said, "I have lost my watch"—Thomas said, "You have hid it
somewhere; I will go up stairs and find it"—she ran up stairs, got the watch and gave it me—the policeman came and said would I give them in charge—I said no, I had got my watch—I did not miss any money—I was not sober.
MATILDA COLLEDGE . I was passing by, and Pingo said, "Your husband is here with a lot of women; they won't allow us to drink with him," and they had sent for a woman to take his watch—he said, "I will see him home."
ROBERT HALL . I am a hairdresser, of 57, Amelia-street—Colledge lodges in my house. At half-past eleven o'clock on this night I heard a scuffle, and heard Colledge tell his wife in the passage that he had lost his gold watch—Thomas said she would find it—I said I would fetch a policeman.
JOHN LEECH ( policeman, P 139). I was called in, and found Colledge and his wife and the policeman up stairs—Colledge said he had lost his watch—Thomas said she had found it.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLER. Q. Did not he say he had lost some money? A. Yes, 24 l., and there was 45 l. found in a pocket-book; and then he said he had lost 12 s. or 14 s.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18481023-2469]
2469. SARAH THOMAS and WILLIAM PINGO were again indicted for stealing 1 brooch, value 1 s.; the goods of James Colledge, from his person.
JAMES COLLEDGE . I missed this brooch after the prisoners were gone—I had it safe just before I lost my watch—I gave the prisoners into custody for stealing it.
ANN THOROGOOD . I am the wife of John Thorogood, a policeman. I searched Thomas, and found a glove in her bosom, with this brooch and 1 s. in it—I said, "What is this?"—she put her hands together, and said, "My God! this is the pin; for God's sake, don't say anything about it; I might have made away with it."
Cross-examined by MR. MELLER. Q. Was Colledge there? A. Yes, and his wife—the prisoner said, "Pray don't say you found it; I had plenty of opportunity of making away with it—I did not, know I had it."
MATILDA COLLEDGE cross-examined. Q. Was not your husband excessively drunk? A. No, he was capable of walking—I assisted him home with Pingo, one on each side—Pingo said, "I shall stick to your husband"—I was looking for my husband when Pingo came and spoke to me—he had this brooch in his shirt—I saw it at the station—I had before seen it in my husband's shirt—he did not fall down—I did not state that I had lost 45 l., or that I missed some sovereigns—I took my husband's watch out of his pocket—Pingo asked to look at it—I saw it safe into his pocket—I said, "He has had a little to drink; if you don't touch him he will take care of it"—I was bathing his face with water to get the blood off—he was very much cut and beaten—Thomas said, "Allow me to hold the basin?" and during that time I think she must have taken the pin; I never saw it afterwards—I had taken the money, hid it, and missed it; no one knew where it was—my husband said he had lost his watch, and Pingo should not leave the house till he found it—my husband was the worse for liquor—he said he was satisfied, and the prisoners went away, and then I missed the brooch.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18481023-2470]
2470. JOHN MILLER , stealing 1/4 lb. weight of tea, and 1 sheet of paper, value 2 s. 5 d.; the goods of James John Charles Child, having been befre convicted.
STEPHEN WENTWORTH . I am in the service of Mr. Child, grocer, of Norwood. On 13th Oct. I was with his cart, at Norwood—I went into a customer's house, and the prisoner was brought there to me by a gentleman, who gave him in charge, and gave me a 1/4 lb. of tea—the prisoner said I was to give him a thrashing, and let him go—it is James John Charles Child's property.
GEORGE ROUS ( policeman). The prisoner was given into ray charge.
JOHN TANNER . I was at my window, and saw the prisoner come away from the cart—he was about twenty yards from it—I went, took hold of him, and the tea dropped from his coat—I picked it up, and gave it to Wentworth.
WILLIAM WESTON ( policeman). I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction—( read—Convicted Jan., 1848, and confined three months and whipped)—I was present—he is the person.
GUILTY †. Aged 16.— Transported for Seven Years .
[trialAccount t18481023-2471]
2471. JOHN BENNETT , stealing 75 yards of ribbon, 3 yards of twist, 3 ounces of silk, and other articles, value 2 l. 2 s. 11 d.; the goods of George Smith, his master.
MR. PAYNE. conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE SMITH . I am a cap-manufacturer, of Union-street, Borough—the prisoner was in my employ—it was his duty to cut out materials for caps, and give them to the cap-maker—he had access to the stock—I received information, and on the 18th Oct. watched him, had him taken in charge, went with the policeman to his house, and found all these things ( produced)—I had sold him none of them, nor authorized him to take them away.
Cross-examined by MR. WOOLLETT. Q. How long had he been in your service? A. About two years—I had no character with him—he said he would be a trustworthy servant, and I took him for the sake of his family—he has been in the habit of working off the premises—I cannot say when the last time was.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you give him these seventy-five yards of ribbon, or the velvet, to make up? A. No—whatever he had was put down by my clerk—I knew when I took him that he had had six months' imprisonment for embezzlement—I took him to give him a chance of redeeming himself.
RACHEL SMITH . I am the wife of the last witness. I did not sell the prisoner seventy-five yards of ribbon, or this lace, silk, or velvet, or authorize him to take them away.
Cross-examined. Q. Other persons serve besides you and your husband? A. It is a wholesale-house—we should not sell a single article, only to the trade—we have a great many persons employed—my husband, myself, and the clerk, are the only persons who sell—the prisoner never had more than twelve caps; that was two or three months ago.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you sell ribbon, or velvet, or any of the things mentoned? A. No, only when made up.
JOHN THOMAS GAIN . I am Mr. Smith's clerk. I did not give the prisoner authority to take any of these articles—he had no right to them without Mr. Smith's permission—he sometimes bought things, and paid for them by instalments—this is a list of the whole of them—( producing it)—none of these things arc mentioned in it.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner has had caps to make at times? A. Yes—there are several females in the establishment—there is a boy named James—he did not sell without Mr. Smith's orders.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you know of the prisoner having any caps to make
which were not brought home? A. No; he had no right to these things on the premises—seventy-five yards of ribbon would not be given out for the purpose of caps—he was authorized to give out materials for making caps.
JOHN WOOLFE ( policeman, M 213). I took the prisoner on 18th Oct.—I went with Mr. Smith to 22, Belvedere-place, Southwark-bridge-road, and found this property produced—Mr. Smith claimed it—the prisoner said the buttons came from Ansell and Elliott's, and that he bought the materials for this child's dress of Mrs. Smith—those were the only things he gave any explanation of.
COURT to MR. SMITH. Q. Had any of your people authority to purchase on their own account? A. Yes, but only those employed on the premises, at cost price; they might pay for it by instalments—an account of such goods is kept—these things are not entered—the prisoner said at the station that he did not know how the ribbon came to his place.
GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18481023-2472]
2472. JOHN BENNETT was again indicted for stealing 3 1/4 yards of lining, value 7 d.; the goods of George Smith, his master.
MR. PAYNE. conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE SMITH . I missed a piece of cotton lining, and found it wrapped up in paper at the side of the prisoner's cutting-board; it measured three yards and a quarter—I put it back again, went into another room, made a hole through, and saw the prisoner give the lining to the porter—I had not sold it him—he had no authority to send it away—I asked him where he had sent the porter—he said, "To Crownson's for two caps"—in going to the station he said, "Oh, Mr. Smith! have mercy upon me; you have had others in your employment who have done worse."
Cross-examined. Q. Had he not authority to send lining away? A. Not unless he asked me if he might make up some caps—he might send it to another person's house to be made up, but not to his own.
RACHEL SMITH . I saw the prisoner roll up some lining in paper—I had not authorized him to cut it off—I told my husband.
GUILTY. Aged 30.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18481023-2473]
2473. MATILDA HARMANT , stealing 20 yards of cotton, value 7 s.; the goods of Benjamin Haydon, having been before convicted.
BENJAMIN HAYDON . I am a linen-draper, of 51, Bermondsey-street. On 24th Oct., about half-past seven o'clock in the morning, I placed this piece of cotton on a rail inside my doorway—I missed it about ten minutes past eleven—it has my private mark on it, and has not been sold—I told the police, and the prisoner was brought in about an hour—I went to the station, and found the print in three pieces.
ALFRED AYLETT ( policeman). I received information, and on 24th Oct., about twelve o'clock, took a pattern of the print, and saw a female offering a piece to pledge at a pawnbroker's in High-street—she said it was given her by her sister—I took her to the station—she gave me information, and I found the prisoner at 8, Red Cross-street, Borough—I said her sister was in custody for stealing some print—she said she knew nothing about it; then she said a woman gave it her, and she gave part of it to her sister to pledge, and the remainder was at home—I went there and found two more pieces—they all corresponded.
Prisoner's Defence. A woman with a baby asked me to hold a bundle while she went into a shop; she did not return, and I took it home.
ROBERT WOOD ( policeman). I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction
—( read, "Convicted July, 1848, confined three months ")—I was present, she is the person.
GUILTY . * Aged 35.— Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18481023-2474]
2474. JOHN PURFLEET and RICHARD FOREMAN , feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Henry Eastwood, and stealing 1 purse, 6 sovereigns, 5 half-sovereigns, 4 half-crowns, 3 s., 5 sixpences, and 7 halfpence, his property.
HARRIET EASTWOOD . I am the wife of Henry Eastwood, who keeps a beer-shop at Mill-pond—it is his dwelling-house—the prisoners formerly lived with us—Purfleet left five or six weeks ago, and Foreman on 27th Sept.—I had no other lodgers. On 24th Oct., about ten o'clock in the morning, Purfleet came and asked me whether my husband was at home—I said, "No"—in about five minutes Foreman came—they asked me for the dominoes, and went into the tap-room—in a few minutes they came to the bar and had a pint of beer, which Purfleet paid for—I went into the kitchen—I could see from there through the bar to the front door—I frequently went backwards and forwards—I went into the tap-room, looked over a small partition, and saw the prisoners in conversation—Foreman then came into the kitchen, and wanted to go up stairs and play at bagatelle—I refused him—he went into the tap-room—Purfleet was there—they stood a few minutes—Purfleet came out, and walked through the passage three times, in conversation with me all the time, close to the kitchen door—I was in the kitchen—I heard the creaking of the stairs door, and footsteps on the stairs—those stairs lead to a long room; then there is another stair-case leading to our bed-room—I pushed by Purfleet, went into the bar, and missed Foreman—I went to the stairs door, found no one there, then went into the bar again, heard footsteps on the stairs, and a creaking of the door, and saw Foreman in the bar, but half a yard from the door, with his back towards it, the door just closing as if some one had come down—I left them there, and went into the kitchen—I did not see them go out, but missed them—in about ten minutes Purfleet came back and spoke to me in the bar about the brewers—my husband came in, and went into the skittle-ground—Purfleet joined him there—Foreman came in and joined them—I had not told my husband what I had seen—they were there till about one o'clock—about six in the evening I went up to my bed-room—it was locked as I had left it in the morning—I missed from my drawers five sovereigns, five half-sovereigns, 8 s. or 9 s. in copper, and a sovereign and some half-crowns from the cash-box—the drawer was not locked—I had seen it safe the night before—I know this halfpenny ( produced) by marks on it, and can swear it was in the drawer—I also swear to this half-crown—the bed-room door key was in my pocket, but the key of the staircase door, which was left in it, will open the bed-room door.
Cross-examined by MR. MELLER. Q. Did many customers came in? A. Only one while the prisoners were there—there is something unusual in this half-crown—I know this halfpenny, because my husband said it was not good, and wished me to save it for the brewers.
HENRY EASTWOOD . I am the husband of the last witness—my house is in the parish of St. Mary Magdalene, Bermondsey. My wife told me something, between six and seven o'clock, and I missed a purse, six sovereigns, five half-sovereigns, four half-crowns, three shillings, five sixpences, seven pence, and three halfpence, from a drawer in my bed-room, which was safe at
eight minutes to ten o'clock that morning—this half-crown and halfpenny were there then—I found the stair-case key, which was in the door, fitted my bed-room door—I went with a policeman, found the prisoners, and gave them in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you add to the money in the drawer from time to time? A. Yes—I went there to put a sovereign in, and take some silver out—when I went again 7 s. or 10 s. were left, but no gold—the gold had been in a purse, and the silver in a cash-box—one sovereign was with the silver—a man lodged in the house—his bed-room is next to mine, but he was out with me at the time—I and my wife had not agreed to do anything with the halfpenny—I have often missed money before, while the prisoners lodged there.
WILLIAM NOAKES ( policeman). Purfleet was given in my charge at Mr. Eastwood's—he said he knew nothing of the robbery, and had not been up stairs that day—I found on him three half-crowns, one of which is identified, two shillings, and a threepenny-piece—I found Foreman in Well-close-place, about twelve o'clock—he said he knew nothing of the robbery, and had not been up stairs that day—I found on him half-a-crown, one shilling, 10 1/2 d. in copper, and the halfpenny which has been identified.
(James Chipperville, a carpenter, and John Page, carter, of James-place, gave Foreman; and Abraham Hide, butcher, of Hackney-road, and George----, baker, of Rotherhithe, gave Purfleet a good character.)
PURFLEET — GUILTY . Aged 22.
FOREMAN — GUILTY . Aged 21
Transported for Ten Years.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, THE 27 TH OF NOVEMBER.
